    *   **Reliance on GPT-4:** The HAI approach depends heavily on GPT-4, which might raise concerns about potential biases or limitations inherent to that specific model. While human feedback is used, the underlying generations are still driven by GPT-4's understanding.

    *   **Limited Dataset Size (potentially):** The paper mentions using 900 instruction proposals and 2172 hypothetical questions which seems like a rather small dataset. While well-curated, increasing the scale could offer a more robust evaluation of MLLMs. It's unclear if the 2172 questions are derived from 947 *different* factoid QA pairings.

    *   **Generalizability of Findings (potential):** While the benchmark demonstrates challenges on *existing* MLLMs, it would be useful to investigate how *specific training strategies* designed to address such biases impact scores in this setting.  Do methods focused on grounding improve performance? Are smaller but well-curated training sets sufficient to overcome the challenge compared to larger, less carefully curated ones?

*   **Potential Influence:** The Chart-HQA benchmark has the potential to become a standard evaluation tool for MLLMs in the chart understanding domain. It could drive the development of more robust and reliable models that are less susceptible to output biases.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of multimodal learning. The HQA task is a well-defined and valuable addition to the evaluation landscape for MLLMs. The HAI data synthesis approach is practical and efficient. However, the relatively small dataset size and dependency on GPT-4 for data generation (potential introduction of bias) are minor limitations. The benchmark highlights an important problem and offers a valuable resource for future research, justifying a high score. There's a significant opportunity for future work to address the dependency on a single LLM for synthesis and scale the data.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Disparities in LLM Reasoning Accuracy and Explanations: A Case Study on African American English
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04099v1)
- **Authors**: Runtao Zhou, Guangya Wan, Saadia Gabriel, Sheng Li, Alexander J Gates, Maarten Sap, Thomas Hartvigsen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in reasoning tasks, leading to their widespread deployment. However, recent studies have highlighted concerning biases in these models, particularly in their handling of dialectal variations like African American English (AAE). In this work, we systematically investigate dialectal disparities in LLM reasoning tasks. We develop an experimental framework comparing LLM performance given Standard American English (SAE) and AAE prompts, combining LLM-based dialect conversion with established linguistic analyses. We find that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler reasoning chains and explanations for AAE inputs compared to equivalent SAE questions, with disparities most pronounced in social science and humanities domains. These findings highlight systematic differences in how LLMs process and reason about different language varieties, raising important questions about the development and deployment of these systems in our multilingual and multidialectal world. Our code repository is publicly available at https://github.com/Runtaozhou/dialect_bias_eval.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) perform differently when reasoning about questions posed in African American English (AAE) compared to Standard American English (SAE).  The authors developed an experimental framework that involves LLM-based dialect conversion to generate AAE versions of questions from established benchmarks. They then compared LLM responses to both SAE and AAE prompts, analyzing accuracy, explanation complexity, readability, and psychological expression. The core finding is that LLMs consistently produce less accurate responses and simpler, potentially biased explanations for AAE inputs. The paper also explores preliminary mitigation strategies using different prompting techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of dialectal disparities across multiple dimensions of LLM reasoning, extending beyond simple accuracy to consider explanation quality and style. While some prior work has explored biases against AAE, this study is more comprehensive in its approach to analyzing different facets of LLM reasoning, including explanation complexity, readability, and cognitive expressions. The development of an LLM-based AAE conversion that leverages linguistic features improves upon existing dialect conversion tools.

**Significance:**

The findings are significant because they highlight how LLMs can perpetuate and amplify linguistic biases, particularly against AAE speakers. As LLMs are increasingly used in high-stakes domains like education and healthcare, understanding and mitigating these biases is crucial for ensuring equitable access and outcomes. The paper also contributes practically by proposing and evaluating preliminary mitigation strategies, providing potential avenues for making LLMs more equitable across dialects. The analysis of conversational norms and their impact on model performance adds another layer of significance. The study’s attention to both accuracy and the quality and style of explanations sets it apart from simpler accuracy-focused studies.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper examines multiple aspects of LLM reasoning, providing a richer picture of dialectal disparities than studies focusing solely on accuracy.
*   **Linguistically Grounded Approach:** The AAE conversion method leverages established linguistic analyses, ensuring the generated AAE is grammatically correct and authentic.
*   **Systematic Methodology:** The experimental framework is well-designed and allows for controlled comparisons between SAE and AAE prompts.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper identifies potential mitigation strategies and provides a valuable resource for developing more equitable LLMs.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper employs human evaluation and automated metrics to validate the performance of the AAE converter and assess the LLM responses.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Scope of Mitigation Strategies:** The paper explores only a limited set of prompting-based mitigation strategies. Other techniques, such as fine-tuning or data augmentation, could potentially be more effective.
*   **AAE Variety Representation:** While the LLM-based conversion improves upon existing methods, it is still possible that the generated AAE does not fully capture the diversity of AAE usage.
*   **Generalizability:** The study focuses primarily on AAE vs SAE and might not generalize to other language dialects or low-resource languages.
*   **Model Reliance on Explanation Data:** The findings are contingent on the availability and quality of explanation data, which is likely to be skewed toward SAE. This could contribute to the observed disparities in the quality of explanations.
*   **Limited Human Evaluation Sample Size**: The human evaluation comparing the conversion methods is based on a relatively small number of samples.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7/10**.

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by systematically investigating dialectal disparities in LLM reasoning. The comprehensive analysis, linguistically grounded approach, and practical implications are all notable strengths. However, the limitations related to the scope of mitigation strategies, AAE variety representation, generalizability, model reliance on explanation data, and human evaluation sample size temper the overall score. Despite these weaknesses, the paper makes a valuable contribution and provides a solid foundation for future research on fairness and equity in LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### LLMs Can Generate a Better Answer by Aggregating Their Own Responses
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04104v1)
- **Authors**: Zichong Li, Xinyu Feng, Yuheng Cai, Zixuan Zhang, Tianyi Liu, Chen Liang, Weizhu Chen, Haoyu Wang, Tuo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities across tasks, yet they often require additional prompting techniques when facing complex problems. While approaches like self-correction and response selection have emerged as popular solutions, recent studies have shown these methods perform poorly when relying on the LLM itself to provide feedback or selection criteria. We argue this limitation stems from the fact that common LLM post-training procedures lack explicit supervision for discriminative judgment tasks. In this paper, we propose Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method that improves answer quality without requiring the model's discriminative capabilities. GSA first samples multiple diverse responses from the LLM, then aggregates them to obtain an improved solution. Unlike previous approaches, our method does not require the LLM to correct errors or compare response quality; instead, it leverages the model's generative abilities to synthesize a new response based on the context of multiple samples. While GSA shares similarities with the self-consistency (SC) approach for response aggregation, SC requires specific verifiable tokens to enable majority voting. In contrast, our approach is more general and can be applied to open-ended tasks. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that GSA effectively improves response quality across various tasks, including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based problems, and open-ended generation tasks such as code synthesis and conversational responses.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLMs Can Generate a Better Answer by Aggregating Their Own Responses":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Generative Self-Aggregation (GSA), a novel prompting method designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) without relying on their discriminative capabilities. GSA operates in two primary steps: first, it generates multiple diverse responses from the LLM to a given query. Then, it leverages these responses as context to prompt the model to synthesize a new, improved solution. The method aims to exploit the generative strengths of LLMs by enabling them to learn from and combine insights from their own diverse attempts, rather than relying on potentially flawed self-assessment mechanisms like self-correction or selection from multiple candidates. The authors evaluate GSA across a variety of tasks, including mathematical reasoning, knowledge-based problems, code synthesis, and conversational responses, comparing it against baselines like greedy decoding, self-correction, and choose-from-N methods. The empirical results demonstrate that GSA can effectively enhance response quality and, in many cases, outperform the baselines, even matching or exceeding the performance of self-consistency on tasks where the latter is applicable.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its approach to LLM improvement by focusing solely on generative capabilities and explicitly avoiding the reliance on LLMs' discriminative abilities. While methods like self-consistency also aggregate multiple responses, GSA differs by synthesizing an entirely new response informed by the context of the initial responses, rather than simply selecting or voting on pre-existing outputs. This allows the model to combine different reasoning paths and pieces of knowledge, leading to a potentially more comprehensive and refined answer.

**Significance:** The significance of GSA stems from its potential to enhance LLM performance in scenarios where self-assessment is unreliable. The experimental results provide compelling evidence that GSA can be effective across a wide range of tasks and model scales. Also, the paper offers empirical evidence that LLMs exhibit more confidence when generating new responses based on multiple attempts as compared to making selections among existing responses. It is important to note that the ability to aggregate information without needing strong selection mechanisms is a desirable property, especially in tasks where there's no clear-cut correct answer.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and well-defined method:** GSA is easy to understand and implement.
*   **Strong empirical validation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across various tasks and models, demonstrating the effectiveness of GSA.
*   **Comparison with relevant baselines:** The comparison against self-correction, choose-from-N, and self-consistency provides a robust evaluation of GSA's merits.
*   **Insightful analysis:** The ablation studies and likelihood distribution analysis offer valuable insights into the workings of GSA.
*   **Focus on generative strength:** By focusing on generative strengths of LLMs, GSA avoids weaknesses associated with models' limited understanding for self-assessment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Increased computational cost:** GSA requires multiple forward passes through the LLM, leading to increased computational cost compared to greedy decoding. While addressed to some extent by limiting the number of forward passes, this remains a relevant consideration.
*   **Prompt sensitivity:** As with any prompting-based method, the performance of GSA may be sensitive to the design of the aggregation prompt. While the paper includes details on prompts used, a more in-depth exploration of prompt engineering strategies for GSA could be beneficial.
*   **Limited comparison against oracle (Best-of-N):** While the paper does present the results of the "Best-of-N" approach, one should acknowledge that the Best-of-N approach is an upper bound, thus there is a limitation to which the GSA's performance can be improved.

**Potential Influence on the Field:**

GSA has the potential to influence the field of LLM prompting techniques by highlighting the importance of leveraging generative capabilities and avoiding reliance on self-assessment. It could also inspire further research into novel aggregation methods that do not require explicit discriminative judgment. The method is practically applicable, which is appealing to the broader machine learning community.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses of the paper, a score of 7 is assigned. The method is novel and well-validated. However, the increased computational cost and prompt sensitivity are limitations. While promising, its impact on the field remains to be seen, warranting a strong but not exceptional score.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### InterChat: Enhancing Generative Visual Analytics using Multimodal Interactions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04110v1)
- **Authors**: Juntong Chen, Jiang Wu, Jiajing Guo, Vikram Mohanty, Xueming Li, Jorge Piazentin Ono, Wenbin He, Liu Ren, Dongyu Liu
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) and generative visual analytics systems has transformed data-driven insights, yet significant challenges persist in accurately interpreting users' analytical and interaction intents. While language inputs offer flexibility, they often lack precision, making the expression of complex intents inefficient, error-prone, and time-intensive. To address these limitations, we investigate the design space of multimodal interactions for generative visual analytics through a literature review and pilot brainstorming sessions. Building on these insights, we introduce a highly extensible workflow that integrates multiple LLM agents for intent inference and visualization generation. We develop InterChat, a generative visual analytics system that combines direct manipulation of visual elements with natural language inputs. This integration enables precise intent communication and supports progressive, visually driven exploratory data analyses. By employing effective prompt engineering, and contextual interaction linking, alongside intuitive visualization and interaction designs, InterChat bridges the gap between user interactions and LLM-driven visualizations, enhancing both interpretability and usability. Extensive evaluations, including two usage scenarios, a user study, and expert feedback, demonstrate the effectiveness of InterChat. Results show significant improvements in the accuracy and efficiency of handling complex visual analytics tasks, highlighting the potential of multimodal interactions to redefine user engagement and analytical depth in generative visual analytics.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, "InterChat: Enhancing Generative Visual Analytics using Multimodal Interactions":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces InterChat, a generative visual analytics system that leverages multimodal interactions (natural language and direct manipulation) to improve communication between users and Large Language Models (LLMs). InterChat aims to address the limitations of language-only interfaces in visual analytics, which can be inefficient and error-prone when conveying complex analytical intents. The system employs a multi-agent LLM architecture for intent inference and visualization generation.  The integration of direct manipulation allows users to refine their queries and visually drive exploratory data analysis.  Evaluations, including usage scenarios, a user study, and expert feedback, demonstrate that InterChat improves accuracy and efficiency in handling complex visual analytics tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of multimodal interaction (specifically direct manipulation on the visualization) within a *generative* visual analytics workflow powered by LLMs.  While LLMs have been used for visualization before, and multimodal interaction is a well-established HCI concept, combining them to create a more intuitive and efficient exploratory analytics experience is a valuable contribution.  The system effectively addresses a recognized problem within the emerging field of LLM-driven visualization. The multi-agent architecture is also a strong point, demonstrating a clever way to parse and utilize the different modalities of input.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the usability and accessibility of visual analytics for a wider range of users.  By allowing users to leverage both natural language and intuitive visual cues, InterChat could enable data exploration and insight generation for individuals who might not be proficient in traditional visual analytics techniques or command-line interaction. Further, by improving accuracy and efficiency, it enables more complex analytical explorations by even expert users.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined design space and clear rationale for multimodal interaction in this context.
    *   Robust evaluation using multiple methods (usage scenarios, user study, expert feedback) which strengthens the confidence in the results.
    *   The multi-agent LLM architecture appears well-reasoned and effective.
    *   The system directly addresses the challenges of NL-only data analysis environments

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LLMs introduces potential limitations: latency, accuracy, and hallucination risks, while acknowledged, need to be explicitly addressed.
    *   Computational capability. It is not clear whether complex data manipulation such as calculations can be performed or not, which may be a limitation in practice.
    *   Scope is limited to a desktop 2D screen. It does not incorporate touch or other input modalities such as voice input.
    *   The evaluation does not thoroughly explore the failure cases to what degree they can be fully mitigated.

*   **Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a well-designed system and a strong evaluation supporting its potential. However, the reliance on LLMs means its functionality will increase in the future. That said, it is a solid step towards a more accessible user experience. In all, a score of 7 reflects a valuable contribution with potential for further improvement.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simple Self Organizing Map with Visual Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04121v1)
- **Authors**: Alan Luo, Kaiwen Yuan
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated exceptional performance in various vision tasks. However, they tend to underperform on smaller datasets due to their inherent lack of inductive biases. Current approaches address this limitation implicitly-often by pairing ViTs with pretext tasks or by distilling knowledge from convolutional neural networks (CNNs) to strengthen the prior. In contrast, Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs), a widely adopted self-supervised framework, are inherently structured to preserve topology and spatial organization, making them a promising candidate to directly address the limitations of ViTs in limited or small training datasets. Despite this potential, equipping SOMs with modern deep learning architectures remains largely unexplored. In this study, we conduct a novel exploration on how Vision Transformers (ViTs) and Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs) can empower each other, aiming to bridge this critical research gap. Our findings demonstrate that these architectures can synergistically enhance each other, leading to significantly improved performance in both unsupervised and supervised tasks. Code will be publicly available.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ViT-SOM, a novel framework that integrates Vision Transformers (ViTs) with Self-Organizing Maps (SOMs). The core idea is to leverage the complementary strengths of each architecture: ViTs offer powerful feature extraction capabilities, while SOMs provide inherent topological preservation as an inductive bias. The paper proposes using ViTs to generate embeddings that are then fed into an SOM layer for self-supervised learning and clustering. It also extends the framework to supervised classification on small datasets.  Experimental results demonstrate improved performance in both unsupervised (clustering on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, USPS) and supervised (classification on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, Flowers17, SVHN) tasks compared to existing methods, including CNN-based SOM variants. The paper highlights the ability of ViT-SOM to achieve better performance with fewer parameters in some cases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of combining ViTs and SOMs is somewhat novel, although not entirely groundbreaking. The paper highlights the existing gap in research exploring this specific combination and contrasts the approach with previous CNN-SOM integration. The key novelty lies in the *specific* architecture ViT-SOM, the use of cosine similarity in the SOM loss, and the empirical validation demonstrating benefits across various datasets.

*   **Significance:**  The paper makes a contribution by addressing a well-known weakness of ViTs: poor performance on small datasets due to a lack of inductive biases.  It presents a method that attempts to directly inject a topological prior using SOMs, which is a conceptually different approach than implicitly learning priors through pretext tasks or knowledge distillation. The improved performance on benchmarks, particularly compared to CNN-SOM hybrids, suggests that ViT-SOM has potential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture and method (ViT-SOM).
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and tasks.
    *   Comparison against relevant baselines, including CNN-SOMs and ViTs.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in performance and parameter efficiency in some scenarios.
    *   Visualizations (UMAP, decoded prototypes) provide insights into the learned representations.
    *   The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The architecture is relatively simple; the improvements, while notable, aren't dramatically transformative.
    *   While the paper claims that it address limitation of ViT on small datasets, the improvement comparing the the ViT-cls baseline are incremental on both small and larger datasets. The claim is less convincing.
    *   The design choices, such as the specific ViT architecture and SOM parameters, are not justified through ablation studies. There is little analysis of the sensitivity of the performance to changes in these parameters. The architecture could be considered a relatively straightforward combination of existing components.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on empirical results.  A deeper theoretical analysis of why the combination of ViTs and SOMs works so effectively would strengthen the contribution.
    *   The limitations of SOMs, especially the sensitivity to initialization, are acknowledged but not addressed in depth. Alternative SOM training procedures could potentially lead to further improvements.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could encourage further research into integrating ViTs with other self-supervised learning techniques and explicitly injecting inductive biases into transformer architectures. It also highlights the potential of SOMs in conjunction with modern deep learning models, moving beyond purely CNN-based SOM hybrids.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

While the paper presents a solid contribution with empirical validation, its novelty is somewhat limited by the relatively straightforward architecture. The improvements in performance are also incremental rather than groundbreaking. The lack of ablation studies or deeper theoretical analysis also detracts from the overall impact. The claim on improving ViTs on small datasets need more convincing support.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Diff-Reg v2: Diffusion-Based Matching Matrix Estimation for Image Matching and 3D Registration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04127v1)
- **Authors**: Qianliang Wu, Haobo Jiang, Yaqing Ding, Lei Luo, Jin Xie, Jian Yang
- **Abstract**: Establishing reliable correspondences is crucial for all registration tasks, including 2D image registration, 3D point cloud registration, and 2D-3D image-to-point cloud registration. However, these tasks are often complicated by challenges such as scale inconsistencies, symmetry, and large deformations, which can lead to ambiguous matches. Previous feature-based and correspondence-based methods typically rely on geometric or semantic features to generate or polish initial potential correspondences. Some methods typically leverage specific geometric priors, such as topological preservation, to devise diverse and innovative strategies tailored to a given enhancement goal, which cannot be exhaustively enumerated. Additionally, many previous approaches rely on a single-step prediction head, which can struggle with local minima in complex matching scenarios. To address these challenges, we introduce an innovative paradigm that leverages a diffusion model in matrix space for robust matching matrix estimation. Our model treats correspondence estimation as a denoising diffusion process in the matching matrix space, gradually refining the intermediate matching matrix to the optimal one. Specifically, we apply the diffusion model in the doubly stochastic matrix space for 3D-3D and 2D-3D registration tasks. In the 2D image registration task, we deploy the diffusion model in a matrix subspace where dual-softmax projection regularization is applied. For all three registration tasks, we provide adaptive matching matrix embedding implementations tailored to the specific characteristics of each task while maintaining a consistent "match-to-warp" encoding pattern. Furthermore, we adopt a lightweight design for the denoising module. In inference, once points or image features are extracted and fixed, this module performs multi-step denoising predictions through reverse sampling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Diff-Reg v2, a novel approach to image matching and 3D registration that uses a diffusion model in the matching matrix space. The core idea is to treat correspondence estimation as a denoising process, iteratively refining a noisy matching matrix towards the optimal one. The authors tailor the diffusion model to handle 3D-3D and 2D-3D registration tasks by working in the doubly stochastic matrix space and to 2D image registration by working in a matrix subspace with dual-softmax projection. They propose adaptive matching matrix embedding implementations suited to each task while consistently encoding "match-to-warp" information. A lightweight denoising module is designed for efficiency, performing multi-step denoising predictions through reverse sampling after initial feature extraction. Experiments on various 2D and 3D registration datasets demonstrate the method's effectiveness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The core idea of applying a diffusion model *directly* in the matching matrix space is novel. Previous diffusion-based registration methods generally operate in the SE(3) space or flow field space. By directly working in the higher-dimensional matrix space, the method has potential to learn more complex relationship than when it operates in SE3 or flow field space.
    *   Adaptive matching matrix embeddings tailored for different registration tasks show good engineering and contribute to the practical application of the framework.
    *   The lightweight denoising module design addresses a key efficiency concern when using diffusion models.
    *   Integrating diffusion models to existing deep learning-based matchers (LOFTR, Leopard, and 2d3d-matr) appears straightforward, the authors, however, demonstrate potential value of this method through experiments.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper addresses important challenges in registration, such as scale inconsistency, large deformations, and ambiguous matches. The use of a diffusion model provides a principled way to navigate complex matching scenarios and escape local minima.
    *   The consistent "match-to-warp" encoding helps to bridge the gap between the matching matrix and geometric transformations, which is vital for successful registration.
    *   Empirical results are strong, showcasing improvements over state-of-the-art methods on standard datasets.
    *   One potential limitation is its relatively high inference time, which stems from the iterative denoising process. However, the authors acknowledge this limitation and suggest that future work on accelerating diffusion models could mitigate it.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach and its motivation.
    *   The method is well-motivated by the shortcomings of existing approaches.
    *   The adaptive design and lightweight denoising module make the framework applicable to different registration tasks and efficient.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive and demonstrates the method's effectiveness on various benchmarks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper lacks strong theoretical analysis of why diffusion in matrix space is especially beneficial. Intuitions and motivations are described, but a more rigorous explanation would strengthen the work.
    *   The inference time is a practical concern, limiting the method's applicability in real-time scenarios. While mentioned, the mitigation through diffusion acceleration techniques is left for future research.
    *   Although the results show improvement, some performance differences in the tables are marginal, raising questions about its general applicability to other datasets.

* **Influence and Scope of Impact:**

The paper contributes to the following:

1.  **Diffusion Model for Image Matching**: This is a promising idea for robustly refining matching results.
2.  **Adaptive design**: Tailoring the pipeline to handle different registration scenarios (2D, 3D, 2D-3D) increases its versatility.

The method provides new avenue of research in both image matching and 3D registration, as it combines the diffusion framework.

**Justification for Score:**

Overall, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field of image matching and 3D registration by introducing a novel and effective diffusion-based framework operating in the matching matrix space.  The novelty, the addressing of relevant challenges, and the solid experimental results support a positive evaluation. However, the higher inference time and limited in-depth analysis of the theoretical foundation are factors which reduce the score. Also, while promising, the performance improvements are only marginal with respect to the other state of the art deep learning based method, which may influence its impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Token-Efficient Long Video Understanding for Multimodal LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04130v1)
- **Authors**: Jindong Jiang, Xiuyu Li, Zhijian Liu, Muyang Li, Guo Chen, Zhiqi Li, De-An Huang, Guilin Liu, Zhiding Yu, Kurt Keutzer, Sungjin Ahn, Jan Kautz, Hongxu Yin, Yao Lu, Song Han, Wonmin Byeon
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in video-based multimodal large language models (Video-LLMs) have significantly improved video understanding by processing videos as sequences of image frames. However, many existing methods treat frames independently in the vision backbone, lacking explicit temporal modeling, which limits their ability to capture dynamic patterns and efficiently handle long videos. To address these limitations, we introduce STORM (\textbf{S}patiotemporal \textbf{TO}ken \textbf{R}eduction for \textbf{M}ultimodal LLMs), a novel architecture incorporating a dedicated temporal encoder between the image encoder and the LLM. Our temporal encoder leverages the Mamba State Space Model to integrate temporal information into image tokens, generating enriched representations that preserve inter-frame dynamics across the entire video sequence. This enriched encoding not only enhances video reasoning capabilities but also enables effective token reduction strategies, including test-time sampling and training-based temporal and spatial pooling, substantially reducing computational demands on the LLM without sacrificing key temporal information. By integrating these techniques, our approach simultaneously reduces training and inference latency while improving performance, enabling efficient and robust video understanding over extended temporal contexts. Extensive evaluations show that STORM achieves state-of-the-art results across various long video understanding benchmarks (more than 5\% improvement on MLVU and LongVideoBench) while reducing the computation costs by up to $8\times$ and the decoding latency by 2.4-2.9$\times$ for the fixed numbers of input frames. Project page is available at https://research.nvidia.com/labs/lpr/storm
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces STORM (Spatiotemporal Token Reduction for Multimodal LLMs), a novel architecture that aims to improve long video understanding in video-based multimodal large language models (Video-LLMs). The key idea is to incorporate a dedicated temporal encoder, specifically leveraging the Mamba State Space Model, between the image encoder and the LLM. This encoder integrates temporal information into the image tokens, generating enriched representations that preserve inter-frame dynamics across the entire video sequence.  STORM also employs token reduction strategies like test-time sampling and training-based temporal and spatial pooling to reduce computational costs on the LLM without losing crucial temporal information. The paper claims that STORM simultaneously reduces training and inference latency while improving performance, enabling efficient and robust video understanding over extended temporal contexts.  Evaluations show state-of-the-art results across long video understanding benchmarks (MLVU, LongVideoBench, VideoMME) while reducing computation costs and decoding latency for fixed numbers of input frames.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating a temporal encoder using Mamba SSM between the image encoder and the LLM is a significant step forward. It addresses a key limitation of existing Video-LLMs, which treat frames independently, lacking explicit temporal modeling. Using Mamba for temporal modeling is promising due to its linear complexity and potential for capturing long-range dependencies. The combination of this encoder with token reduction techniques (temporal and spatial pooling) to manage computational costs is also valuable.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very important and challenging problem in the field: efficient long video understanding. Overcoming the computational bottlenecks associated with long videos is critical for real-world applications.  The reported improvements in performance and reductions in latency are compelling and demonstrate the practical benefits of the STORM architecture. The benchmarks used (MLVU, LongVideoBench, VideoMME, EgoSchema) are well-established and relevant for evaluating long-video understanding. Showing state-of-the-art performance while significantly reducing computation makes this quite significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture with a good explanation of the Mamba-based temporal encoder.
    *   Effective token reduction strategies to manage computational costs.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation on multiple datasets.
    *   Significant performance improvements compared to existing methods.
    * Thorough ablation studies demonstrate the contribution of various components.
    * Addresses an important problem with significant practical implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper does not provide as much detail as would be ideal about hyperparameter tuning and architecture choices for the Mamba SSM in the temporal encoder. More ablation studies focusing on specific choices regarding the Mamba layer could be informative.
    * The computational efficiency claims, while supported by reported numbers, could be strengthened by also including concrete energy usage data alongside latency measurements to provide a better assessment of its overall environmental impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of Video-LLMs. The STORM architecture provides a viable solution for efficient long video understanding. Other researchers might adopt or adapt this approach. The token reduction strategies presented could also become common practice in the field. In general, the ideas could influence the development of future generations of efficient and high-performing Video-LLMs. The success in integrating temporal modeling so early into the pipeline may lead to further research along these lines.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8/10. The paper presents a novel and well-engineered architecture (STORM) that effectively addresses the challenge of efficient long video understanding in Video-LLMs. The use of Mamba SSM for temporal modeling, combined with carefully designed token reduction techniques, leads to substantial performance improvements and computational savings. While minor aspects related to Mamba architecture choices and more detailed energy usage data might be explored further, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The paper is likely to have a notable and positive influence on the field.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Biological Sequence with Language Model Prompting: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04135v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyue Jiang, Zikang Wang, Yuheng Shan, Heyan Chai, Jiayi Li, Zixian Ma, Xinrui Zhang, Yu Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for addressing challenges across diverse domains. Notably, recent studies have demonstrated that large language models significantly enhance the efficiency of biomolecular analysis and synthesis, attracting widespread attention from academics and medicine. In this paper, we systematically investigate the application of prompt-based methods with LLMs to biological sequences, including DNA, RNA, proteins, and drug discovery tasks. Specifically, we focus on how prompt engineering enables LLMs to tackle domain-specific problems, such as promoter sequence prediction, protein structure modeling, and drug-target binding affinity prediction, often with limited labeled data. Furthermore, our discussion highlights the transformative potential of prompting in bioinformatics while addressing key challenges such as data scarcity, multimodal fusion, and computational resource limitations. Our aim is for this paper to function both as a foundational primer for newcomers and a catalyst for continued innovation within this dynamic field of study.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty and significance score.

**Summary**

The paper is a survey of the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) and prompt engineering techniques to biological sequence analysis. It covers DNA, RNA, protein, and drug discovery tasks, outlining how prompt-based methods can address domain-specific problems, particularly in scenarios with limited labeled data. The survey discusses various prompting strategies, examines the influence of AlphaFold and ESM, and identifies key challenges like data scarcity, multimodal fusion, and computational limitations.  It concludes by proposing future research directions focusing on data-centric annotation, multimodal prompt fusion, and efficient adaptation techniques.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper effectively covers a broad range of applications of LLMs in bioinformatics, providing a valuable overview of the current landscape.
    *   **Clear Organization:** The structure is well-defined, making it easy to navigate the various topics and applications. The use of figures (e.g., timelines, application diagrams, and a taxonomy) enhances understanding.
    *   **Focus on Prompt Engineering:** The emphasis on prompt engineering is timely and relevant, given its crucial role in adapting LLMs to specific tasks, especially when data is limited.
    *   **Identified Challenges and Future Directions:**  The authors clearly outline the key challenges and suggest promising future research directions, providing guidance for researchers in the field. The categorization of future research directions, specifically, is well-done.
    *   **Practical Examples:**  The inclusion of specific examples of how biological tasks can be reformulated as NLP problems with prompts is helpful for readers seeking to apply these techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Depth of Technical Detail:** While the survey provides a good overview, it necessarily lacks deep technical detail on specific methods. It would be useful to have additional details about how the performance results are obtained.
    *   **Limited Critical Analysis of Results:** The survey predominantly describes methods and their applications without providing sufficient comparative analysis of performance or limitations. A more critical evaluation of the reported results, including potential biases or inconsistencies, would enhance the paper's value. While there is discussion of limitations (data scarcity, computational requirements, etc.), it is not really tied to specific methods.
    *   **Novelty of Survey:** It is worth noting that there are already surveys of applications of AI or ML techniques within bioinformatics; however, it could be argued that this is the first survey to focus specifically on LLMs and prompt engineering.
    *   **Limited discussion of benchmarks:** There is not a clear discussion of benchmark datasets or evaluation methodologies for LLM application in bioinformatics, which would make it easier to understand results across papers.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   While the survey is generally comprehensive and well-written, its novelty is arguably limited by existing surveys of ML/AI techniques in bioinformatics. However, the focus specifically on LLMs and prompt engineering is timely and justifies the contribution of this paper. While the individual applications are known in the context of LLMs, a unified discussion highlighting how LLMs are useful and the limitations they have is useful. The clear explanation of limitations of the method and future work is quite valuable. The taxonomy of applications of LLMs in computational biology is also a valuable contribution to the field.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    *   The survey can serve as a valuable resource for researchers new to the field, providing a broad overview of the landscape. It could also stimulate further research by highlighting promising areas for future investigation.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper is a well-organized and comprehensive survey of the application of LLMs in biological sequence analysis, with a particular focus on prompt engineering. It provides a valuable overview of the field, highlights key challenges, and suggests future research directions. The novelty lies primarily in the specific focus on LLMs and prompt engineering, which is a rapidly evolving area with limited survey literature. Although some of the discussed methodologies are already known, the synthesis of information, identification of challenges, and proposed future directions enhance the paper's significance. However, the absence of comparative analysis of the methods, lack of technical depth, and limited benchmark or performance information, keep the score from being higher. It is a good starting point for new researchers but requires further development to provide significant insights and become an authoritative reference.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dynamic Benchmarking of Reasoning Capabilities in Code Large Language Models Under Data Contamination
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04149v1)
- **Authors**: Simin Chen, Pranav Pusarla, Baishakhi Ray
- **Abstract**: The rapid evolution of code largelanguage models underscores the need for effective and transparent benchmarking of their reasoning capabilities. However, the current benchmarking approach heavily depends on publicly available, human-created datasets. The widespread use of these fixed benchmark datasets makes the benchmarking process to be static and thus particularly susceptible to data contamination, an unavoidable consequence of the extensive data collection processes used to train Code LLMs. Existing approaches that address data contamination often suffer from human effort limitations and imbalanced problem complexity. To tackle these challenges, we propose \tool, a novel benchmarking suite for evaluating Code LLMs under potential data contamination. Given a seed programming problem, \tool employs multiple agents to extract and modify the context without altering the core logic, generating semantically equivalent variations. We introduce a dynamic data generation methods and conduct empirical studies on two seed datasets across 21 Code LLMs. Results show that \tool effectively benchmarks reasoning capabilities under contamination risks while generating diverse problem sets to ensure consistent and reliable evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DyCodeEval paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DyCodeEval, a dynamic benchmarking suite for evaluating code large language models (LLMs) in the face of potential data contamination. Unlike static benchmarks, DyCodeEval dynamically generates semantically diverse, yet complexity-controlled programming problems from a seed problem using LLM-based agents. The method involves multiple agents that propose scenarios, generate contexts, rewrite prompts, and validate the new problems, ensuring semantic equivalence and consistency. The authors conduct experiments on contaminated models and real-world code LLMs, demonstrating that DyCodeEval provides a more reliable assessment of reasoning capabilities than static benchmarks and mitigates the impact of data contamination. They introduce a new metric, DivPass, to measure the effect and benefits of the diversified dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The core idea of dynamically generating benchmarks is innovative. The concept of separating context and algorithm and leveraging LLMs to generate variations within the context while preserving algorithmic complexity is a valuable contribution. It's a significant step beyond simply crawling existing problems from the web (which might also be contaminated). The use of a multi-agent system to automate the process also reduces reliance on manual effort, which is a key limiting factor in many existing approaches. The introduction of a new metric, DivPass, is a step in the right direction to evaluate data contamination.

**Significance:**

The problem of data contamination in LLM benchmarking is critical and well-recognized. This paper tackles the problem head-on by introducing a framework that is robust to such contamination. The paper presents convincing evidence that DyCodeEval reveals performance degradations obscured by traditional static benchmarks, which is an important finding. It has the potential to influence how code LLMs are evaluated and developed in the future. The study results on open and closed models help provide confidence in the robustness of the method.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a key problem:** Data contamination is a major obstacle to accurate LLM evaluation.
*   **Novel methodology:** The dynamic generation approach is innovative and well-structured.
*   **Automated process:** Reduces reliance on manual effort and scales well.
*   **Empirical validation:** Experiments on contaminated and real-world models provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of DyCodeEval.
*   **Clear explanation:** The paper is well-written and explains the methodology and findings clearly.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:** The reliance on LLMs for problem generation introduces a significant computational cost. The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't offer substantial solutions. Running DyCodeEval might be prohibitively expensive for some researchers.
*   **Dependence on the foundation LLM:** The quality of the generated problems hinges on the capabilities of the underlying LLM (e.g., CLAUDE-3.5-SONNET). If the foundation LLM has biases or limitations, they could be reflected in the generated benchmarks. Experiments related to foundation model are limited and thus provide minimal confidence.
*   **Complexity control:** While the paper emphasizes complexity preservation, the metric used could be improved. Algorithmic complexity is hard to directly measure. There may be ways to get better signals for equivalent complexity, potentially by monitoring code structure or AST (Abstract Syntax Tree) properties.
*   **Human Verification:** A more robust human validation process, maybe with well designed rubrics, would provide stronger confidence on the generated dataset.

**Justification of Score:**

DyCodeEval represents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM benchmarking, particularly in the context of data contamination. While there are limitations related to computational cost and dependence on the quality of the LLM, the proposed dynamic benchmark generation method is both novel and significant. It has the potential to improve the reliability and transparency of LLM evaluations, promoting more accurate assessments of model capabilities. However, some aspects, specially the consistency rate and model choice, could have been better discussed. The reliance on a high-quality LLM, the reliance of the results from contamination models, and the not-as-robust human verification limit its broad applicability, lowering the score slightly.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ticktack : Long Span Temporal Alignment of Large Language Models Leveraging Sexagenary Cycle Time Expression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04150v1)
- **Authors**: Xue Han, Qian Hu, Yitong Wang, Wenchun Gao, Lianlian Zhang, Qing Wang, Lijun Mei, Chao Deng, Junlan Feng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) suffer from temporal misalignment issues especially across long span of time. The issue arises from knowing that LLMs are trained on large amounts of data where temporal information is rather sparse over long times, such as thousands of years, resulting in insufficient learning or catastrophic forgetting by the LLMs. This paper proposes a methodology named "Ticktack" for addressing the LLM's long-time span misalignment in a yearly setting. Specifically, we first propose to utilize the sexagenary year expression instead of the Gregorian year expression employed by LLMs, achieving a more uniform distribution in yearly granularity. Then, we employ polar coordinates to model the sexagenary cycle of 60 terms and the year order within each term, with additional temporal encoding to ensure LLMs understand them. Finally, we present a temporal representational alignment approach for post-training LLMs that effectively distinguishes time points with relevant knowledge, hence improving performance on time-related tasks, particularly over a long period. We also create a long time span benchmark for evaluation. Experimental results prove the effectiveness of our proposal.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Ticktack," a novel methodology aimed at improving the long-span temporal reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). LLMs often struggle with temporal misalignment due to the sparse distribution of temporal information in their training data (covering vast periods but concentrated in recent times). Ticktack addresses this by:

1.  **Sexagenary Cycle Representation:** Instead of using Gregorian years (which have an uneven distribution), it represents time using the sexagenary cycle (a 60-year cycle), leading to a more uniform temporal distribution.
2.  **Polar Coordinate Encoding:**  It encodes the sexagenary cycle using polar coordinates to capture the cyclic nature of time and the relative order within each cycle.
3.  **Temporal Alignment:** A post-training process is introduced to align the LLM's weight space with the sexagenary cycle representation, enhancing the model's ability to distinguish and relate information across time.
4.  **TempLS Benchmark:**  The paper also introduces a new long-span temporal benchmark dataset, TempLS, spanning from 75,000 BCE to 2025 AD, to evaluate the effectiveness of Ticktack.  Experiments are conducted on several open-source LLMs, demonstrating improved performance on time-related tasks compared to baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of the sexagenary cycle is interesting, particularly given the cultural context of the authors.  The polar coordinate encoding is a decent way to represent this cycle in a manner that the model can understand. Temporal alignment via Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC) is not particularly novel in itself, as EWC has been used in various continual learning contexts. However, its application in conjunction with the sexagenary cycle representation is a distinct approach for temporal alignment in LLMs. The TempLS dataset appears to be a useful contribution, given the scarcity of long-span temporal benchmarks.

*   **Significance:**  Temporal reasoning is a well-known weakness of LLMs, and improving this capability is valuable. If the results are robust, this work can provide a pathway to improved modeling. It does seem that the improvements are significant based on the data in Table 1.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The sexagenary cycle representation is a clever solution to the sparse data problem.
    *   The TempLS dataset is a valuable resource for future research.
    *   The paper provides a clear methodology and experimental results.
    *   The improvements over strong baseline models seems signficant
    *   The ablation study shows improvements when using sexagenary temporal embedding

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The choice of the sexagenary cycle might limit the generalizability of the approach. Would it work as well for languages and cultures that don't have this tradition?
    *   While EWC is a reasonable approach for temporal alignment, the paper could benefit from exploring and comparing with alternative knowledge editing or continual learning techniques.
    *   The experiments are limited to open-source LLMs and Chinese-centric datasets (Baidu Baike derived TempLS). It is unclear if the results would generalize to more diverse datasets and proprietary LLMs.
    *   The paper does not compare Ticktack to any existing, time-related finetuning approaches
    *   Given how common LoRA is, there are potentially more efficient methods to train the model than full gradient updates with EWC

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on temporal reasoning in LLMs.  The TempLS dataset could become a standard benchmark.  The sexagenary cycle idea may inspire other culturally informed approaches to address similar issues in LLMs.  However, the limitations described above need to be addressed to ensure the long-term impact.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to a significant problem (temporal reasoning in LLMs), and provides a new resource (TempLS). However, there is limited comparison to prior methods, a reliance on Chinese-specific data and the sexagenary system for time embedding, and the algorithmic choices used are not particularly unique. Given these factors, the paper makes a moderately significant contribution, and the score reflects this. It has the potential to inspire future work, but further research is needed to address the limitations and fully realize its impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### KidneyTalk-open: No-code Deployment of a Private Large Language Model with Medical Documentation-Enhanced Knowledge Database for Kidney Disease
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04153v1)
- **Authors**: Yongchao Long, Chao Yang, Gongzheng Tang, Jinwei Wang, Zhun Sui, Yuxi Zhou, Shenda Hong, Luxia Zhang
- **Abstract**: Privacy-preserving medical decision support for kidney disease requires localized deployment of large language models (LLMs) while maintaining clinical reasoning capabilities. Current solutions face three challenges: 1) Cloud-based LLMs pose data security risks; 2) Local model deployment demands technical expertise; 3) General LLMs lack mechanisms to integrate medical knowledge. Retrieval-augmented systems also struggle with medical document processing and clinical usability. We developed KidneyTalk-open, a desktop system integrating three technical components: 1) No-code deployment of state-of-the-art (SOTA) open-source LLMs (such as DeepSeek-r1, Qwen2.5) via local inference engine; 2) Medical document processing pipeline combining context-aware chunking and intelligent filtering; 3) Adaptive Retrieval and Augmentation Pipeline (AddRep) employing agents collaboration for improving the recall rate of medical documents. A graphical interface was designed to enable clinicians to manage medical documents and conduct AI-powered consultations without technical expertise. Experimental validation on 1,455 challenging nephrology exam questions demonstrates AddRep's effectiveness: achieving 29.1% accuracy (+8.1% over baseline) with intelligent knowledge integration, while maintaining robustness through 4.9% rejection rate to suppress hallucinations. Comparative case studies with the mainstream products (AnythingLLM, Chatbox, GPT4ALL) demonstrate KidneyTalk-open's superior performance in real clinical query. KidneyTalk-open represents the first no-code medical LLM system enabling secure documentation-enhanced medical Q&A on desktop. Its designs establishes a new framework for privacy-sensitive clinical AI applications. The system significantly lowers technical barriers while improving evidence traceability, enabling more medical staff or patients to use SOTA open-source LLMs conveniently.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "KidneyTalk-open: No-code Deployment of a Private Large Language Model with Medical Documentation-Enhanced Knowledge Database for Kidney Disease":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KidneyTalk-open, a desktop system designed to provide privacy-preserving medical decision support for kidney disease. It addresses challenges related to deploying large language models (LLMs) in medical settings, such as data security concerns with cloud-based LLMs, the technical expertise required for local deployments, and the lack of mechanisms to integrate medical knowledge into general LLMs. KidneyTalk-open offers a no-code deployment solution for open-source LLMs (like DeepSeek-r1 and Qwen2.5) with a medical documentation-enhanced knowledge database. It features a context-aware document processing pipeline and an Adaptive Retrieval and Augmentation Pipeline (AddRep) for improved medical document recall. The system has a graphical interface to facilitate usage by clinicians without technical expertise. The paper evaluates AddRep's performance on a nephrology exam question dataset and compares it to existing localized LLM applications in a case study on diabetic kidney disease.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the no-code deployment aspect combined with a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system specifically tailored for the medical domain. While RAG systems and local LLM deployment are not individually novel, KidneyTalk-open's user-friendly interface, its focus on privacy-preserving medical applications, and the multi-agent approach (AddRep)  are noteworthy. However, it is essential to compare it against similar applications already publicly available. If there are already many such "no-code" solutions for the deployment of private LLMs on a local system, the novelty decreases significantly.
*   **Significance:**  The potential significance is substantial. Democratizing access to LLMs for medical professionals without requiring extensive technical knowledge is a valuable goal. If successfully adopted, it could improve decision support, enhance efficiency, and potentially lead to better patient outcomes. The emphasis on privacy is also crucial, given the sensitive nature of medical data. However, the paper needs to demonstrate the usability and real-world impact of the system through user studies involving clinicians.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Real-World Problem:** Tackles the practical challenges of deploying LLMs in healthcare.
    *   **No-Code Approach:** Lowers the barrier to entry for medical professionals.
    *   **Privacy-Preserving Design:** Addresses a critical concern in healthcare applications.
    *   **Multi-Agent Collaboration (AddRep):**  A promising approach for improving medical document recall.
    *   **Clear Evaluation Methodology:**  Uses a nephrology exam dataset and comparative case studies.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The system is specifically tailored for kidney disease. The effort to generalize it to other medical domains might be extensive.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Metrics:** The CNME-MCQ only provides a measure of "correctness" in a test setting. Real-world utility involves more nuanced factors. A proper usability study on clinical settings is absent.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Existing RAG Frameworks:** The paper should perform a comparison with other well-established RAG libraries or pipelines that use the same datasets.
    *   **Scalability and Maintenance:**  Long-term maintainability of medical knowledge databases and continuous updates to guidelines are not sufficiently addressed. The cost of adapting to changing medical knowledge is a crucial factor.
    *   **Performance Bottlenecks:** Desktop performance can be highly variable depending on hardware capabilities. A detailed analysis of memory footprint and runtime requirements for different LLMs would be useful.
    *   **Missing Error Analysis:** Detailed insights into where the system is making mistakes are missing. Which kind of questions does AddRep frequently get wrong? This information would allow the authors and others to iterate on the system further.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If KidneyTalk-open proves to be effective and user-friendly, it could influence the development of similar LLM-based decision support systems in other medical specialties. The multi-agent approach could inspire further research in improving RAG systems. However, without broader adoption and demonstrable impact on clinical practice, its influence will remain limited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by providing a practical, privacy-focused solution for deploying LLMs in medical decision support. The no-code interface is a significant advantage, and the multi-agent AddRep approach shows promise for improving medical document recall.

However, several weaknesses need to be addressed before the system can have a significant impact. The specialization towards kidney disease limits the application range. The evaluation lacks real-world clinical usability studies, scalability or maintenance aspects are missing and performance bottlenecks are not thoroughly investigated. Due to these limitations, the score will be reduced.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning for Sequential Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04162v1)
- **Authors**: Ziqiang Cui, Yunpeng Weng, Xing Tang, Xiaokun Zhang, Dugang Liu, Shiwei Li, Peiyang Liu, Bowei He, Weihong Luo, Xiuqiang He, Chen Ma
- **Abstract**: Sequential recommendation aims to model user preferences based on historical behavior sequences, which is crucial for various online platforms. Data sparsity remains a significant challenge in this area as most users have limited interactions and many items receive little attention. To mitigate this issue, contrastive learning has been widely adopted. By constructing positive sample pairs from the data itself and maximizing their agreement in the embedding space,it can leverage available data more effectively. Constructing reasonable positive sample pairs is crucial for the success of contrastive learning. However, current approaches struggle to generate reliable positive pairs as they either rely on representations learned from inherently sparse collaborative signals or use random perturbations which introduce significant uncertainty. To address these limitations, we propose a novel approach named Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning (SRA-CL), which leverages semantic information to improve the reliability of contrastive samples. SRA-CL comprises two main components: (1) Cross-Sequence Contrastive Learning via User Semantic Retrieval, which utilizes large language models (LLMs) to understand diverse user preferences and retrieve semantically similar users to form reliable positive samples through a learnable sample synthesis method; and (2) Intra-Sequence Contrastive Learning via Item Semantic Retrieval, which employs LLMs to comprehend items and retrieve similar items to perform semantic-based item substitution, thereby creating semantically consistent augmented views for contrastive learning. SRA-CL is plug-and-play and can be integrated into standard sequential recommendation models. Extensive experiments on four public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel framework called Semantic Retrieval Augmented Contrastive Learning (SRA-CL) for sequential recommendation. Addressing the data sparsity challenge in sequential recommendation, SRA-CL uses large language models (LLMs) to generate more reliable contrastive samples for both cross-sequence and intra-sequence contrastive learning. It consists of two main components: 1) Cross-Sequence Contrastive Learning via User Semantic Retrieval, which uses LLMs to understand user preferences and retrieve semantically similar users to form positive samples; and 2) Intra-Sequence Contrastive Learning via Item Semantic Retrieval, which employs LLMs to comprehend items and retrieve similar items for semantic-based item substitution.  The framework is designed to be plug-and-play and can be integrated into standard sequential recommendation models. Extensive experiments on multiple public datasets demonstrate the effectiveness and generalizability of the proposed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to improve the quality of contrastive samples in sequential recommendation is relatively novel. Existing methods often rely on representations learned from sparse collaborative signals or use random perturbations, which are suboptimal. SRA-CL tackles this by leveraging the rich semantic information available in textual data to augment the construction of positive sample pairs. However, the high-level idea of using LLMs to inject semantic information into recommender systems is not entirely new, with several recent works exploring similar avenues. The novelty lies specifically in *how* the LLMs are used to generate contrastive samples in a sequential recommendation setting. The separation into cross-sequence and intra-sequence contrastive learning modules with tailored LLM usage is a positive aspect. The user semantic retrieval module with a learnable sample synthesis component offers a good contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in sequential recommendation: data sparsity.  By improving the reliability of contrastive samples, SRA-CL demonstrates enhanced performance on benchmark datasets. The model-agnostic nature of the framework is also a significant advantage, allowing it to be readily integrated into existing recommendation pipelines.  The performance improvements reported are meaningful and provide evidence for the efficacy of the proposed approach. The ablation studies provide a good understanding of how each component contributes to the overall performance. The exploration of hyperparameters provides valuable insights for practical applications.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Relevance:** The paper addresses a fundamental challenge in sequential recommendation.
    * **Novel Approach:** The use of LLMs for semantic retrieval to augment contrastive sample generation is innovative.
    * **Model Agnostic:**  The framework can be integrated with existing recommendation models.
    * **Empirical Validation:** Extensive experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of SRA-CL.
    * **Ablation Studies:** Thorough ablation analysis clarifies the contribution of individual components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM Cost & API Dependency:**  The approach relies on calling external LLM APIs, which can be costly and introduce latency, even during training. This is a significant practical consideration that is not fully addressed, though the inference avoids LLM calls. The current implementation is dependent on the availability and stability of these APIs, which are potential points of failure.
    * **Limited LLM Usage Discussion:**  While the paper utilizes LLMs, there could have been further insights into the choice of specific LLM APIs and a more nuanced discussion of LLM-related hyperparameters (beyond temperature). Different LLMs might affect performance considerably, and the paper doesn't discuss alternative LLMs or their potential effects.
    * **Incremental over Previous Work:** While there's novelty in the contrastive learning augmentation approach, the overarching framework of using LLMs to improve recommendations is an active area, making the contribution more incremental than groundbreaking.  The method leverages existing LLM capabilities without significantly advancing LLM techniques themselves.
    * **Dataset limitations**: The experiments are performed on standard recommendation datasets. It would add to the impact if they tested this on datasets with more real-world features or where they could examine and analyze the summaries generated by the LLMs.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in sequential recommendation by highlighting the value of leveraging semantic information through LLMs to improve contrastive learning. It could inspire the development of other techniques for augmenting contrastive samples and designing more robust sequential recommendation models.

**Overall Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses discussed above, I assign a score of **7**.  While the paper provides a solid contribution with a novel and effective approach to improving contrastive learning in sequential recommendation, the reliance on external LLM APIs and a degree of incremental novelty limit its overall impact. The paper also doesn't address several critical issues around cost and scalability of using LLM APIs, which reduce the overall practical value.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### TIMER: Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04176v1)
- **Authors**: Hejie Cui, Alyssa Unell, Bowen Chen, Jason Alan Fries, Emily Alsentzer, Sanmi Koyejo, Nigam Shah
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as promising tools for assisting in medical tasks, yet processing Electronic Health Records (EHRs) presents unique challenges due to their longitudinal nature. While LLMs' capabilities to perform medical tasks continue to improve, their ability to reason over temporal dependencies across multiple patient visits and time frames remains unexplored. We introduce TIMER (Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records), a framework that incorporate instruction-response pairs grounding to different parts of a patient's record as a critical dimension in both instruction evaluation and tuning for longitudinal clinical records. We develop TIMER-Bench, the first time-aware benchmark that evaluates temporal reasoning capabilities over longitudinal EHRs, as well as TIMER-Instruct, an instruction-tuning methodology for LLMs to learn reasoning over time. We demonstrate that models fine-tuned with TIMER-Instruct improve performance by 7.3% on human-generated benchmarks and 9.2% on TIMER-Bench, indicating that temporal instruction-tuning improves model performance for reasoning over EHR.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TIMER: Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new framework, TIMER (Temporal Instruction Modeling and Evaluation for Longitudinal Clinical Records), to address the challenges of temporal reasoning in large language models (LLMs) when applied to Electronic Health Records (EHRs). Recognizing that existing benchmarks often overlook the longitudinal and time-dependent nature of EHR data, the authors propose two key components: TIMER-Bench, a new benchmark for evaluating temporal reasoning capabilities, and TIMER-Instruct, an instruction-tuning methodology designed to improve LLMs' ability to reason over time. TIMER-Bench includes queries requiring reasoning across multiple time points within a patient's record. TIMER-Instruct uses model-generated instruction-response pairs grounded to specific parts of the EHR to facilitate temporal-aware learning. The paper demonstrates that models fine-tuned with TIMER-Instruct show improved performance on both human-generated benchmarks and TIMER-Bench, indicating enhanced capabilities for reasoning over EHRs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its explicit focus on temporal reasoning within the context of EHRs. While there's existing work on applying LLMs to healthcare and instruction tuning, this work specifically addresses the gaps in evaluating and improving temporal understanding across longitudinal patient records. TIMER-Bench is a novel benchmark and TIMER-Instruct is a novel methodology to address specific temporal reasoning shortcomings, thus the paper exhibits considerable novelty.

*   **Significance:** The ability of LLMs to effectively reason over temporal dependencies in EHRs has significant implications for various healthcare applications. Improved temporal reasoning can potentially enhance disease management, care planning, and patient history synthesis. By addressing the limitations of existing benchmarks and providing a targeted instruction-tuning approach, this paper contributes to the development of more practical and clinically useful LLMs in healthcare. Specifically, this is highly relevant given the recent trend of large-context LLMs in healthcare applications. The systematic approach of identifying temporal distribution gaps and addressing them through controlled instruction tuning is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and articulates the limitations of existing benchmarks and the importance of temporal reasoning in EHRs.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The TIMER framework offers a well-defined and structured approach to both evaluating and improving temporal reasoning capabilities.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The authors conduct thorough experiments on both human-curated and model-generated benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of TIMER-Instruct.
    *   **Addresses a Critical Gap:** The work fills an important gap in the existing research landscape by focusing on the often-overlooked temporal dimension in EHR analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model-Generated Data:** While leveraging LLMs to generate instruction-response pairs is practical, there is inherent risk of introducing bias or artifacts into the training data. The paper addresses this through clinical validation, but the potential impact of synthetic data remains a consideration.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The focus on a specific dataset from a single medical center may limit the generalizability of the findings. Evaluating the framework on diverse EHR datasets would strengthen the conclusions.
