- **Score**: 7/10

### LEDiT: Your Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer without Positional Encoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04344v1)
- **Authors**: Shen Zhang, Yaning Tan, Siyuan Liang, Linze Li, Ge Wu, Yuhao Chen, Shuheng Li, Zhenyu Zhao, Caihua Chen, Jiajun Liang, Yao Tang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion transformers(DiTs) struggle to generate images at resolutions higher than their training resolutions. The primary obstacle is that the explicit positional encodings(PE), such as RoPE, need extrapolation which degrades performance when the inference resolution differs from training. In this paper, we propose a Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer(LEDiT), a simple yet powerful architecture to overcome this limitation. LEDiT needs no explicit PEs, thereby avoiding extrapolation. The key innovations of LEDiT are introducing causal attention to implicitly impart global positional information to tokens, while enhancing locality to precisely distinguish adjacent tokens. Experiments on 256x256 and 512x512 ImageNet show that LEDiT can scale the inference resolution to 512x512 and 1024x1024, respectively, while achieving better image quality compared to current state-of-the-art length extrapolation methods(NTK-aware, YaRN). Moreover, LEDiT achieves strong extrapolation performance with just 100K steps of fine-tuning on a pretrained DiT, demonstrating its potential for integration into existing text-to-image DiTs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LEDIT: Your Length-Extrapolatable Diffusion Transformer without Positional Encoding":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LEDIT, a novel diffusion transformer architecture designed to generate high-quality images at resolutions exceeding the training resolution, without relying on explicit positional encodings (PEs).  The key innovation lies in leveraging causal attention, which, the authors argue and demonstrate, implicitly encodes positional information through variance changes of the causal attention outputs. They also introduce a multi-dilation convolution training strategy for finer details.  The authors demonstrate through experiments on ImageNet that LEDIT outperforms existing length extrapolation methods like NTK-aware and YaRN, achieving better image quality at higher resolutions (up to 4x). They further show strong performance with fine-tuning on pre-trained DiT models, suggesting ease of integration with existing diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using causal attention to implicitly encode positional information in diffusion transformers is relatively novel. While causal attention itself isn't new, its application and theoretical justification for length extrapolation in DiTs are interesting.  The combination with multi-dilation convolutions provides a pragmatic improvement for detailed image generation. A notable contribution is the theoretical justification demonstrating how variance changes due to causal attention can implicitly encode token position.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a crucial limitation of DiTs: their difficulty in generalizing to higher resolutions than those encountered during training.  Length extrapolation is an essential capability for practical applications of generative models. By eliminating the need for explicit PEs and offering a relatively simple architectural change, LEDIT offers a practical solution that can be potentially adopted by the broader diffusion modeling community. The fine-tuning experiments are significant, as they suggest the method can be easily adapted to existing pre-trained models, reducing the training cost for high-resolution generation. However, many alternative methods exists to address the resolution extrapolation problem.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental validation on ImageNet, demonstrating superior performance compared to state-of-the-art length extrapolation methods. The results are quantitative (FID, IS scores) and qualitative (visual comparisons).
    * **Theoretical Justification:** The authors provide a theoretical argument (Theorem 3.1) for why causal attention can implicitly encode positional information, increasing the credibility of the approach.
    * **Simplicity:**  The proposed modifications to the DiT architecture are relatively simple and readily implementable.
    * **Integration Potential:** The fine-tuning experiments highlight the potential for easy integration with existing pre-trained diffusion models.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:**  The experiments are primarily focused on ImageNet. It's unclear how well LEDIT would generalize to other datasets or more complex generative tasks.
    * **Variance Derivation Assumptions:**  The theoretical justification relies on certain assumptions (e.g., independent and identically distributed values, low correlations). Deviations from these assumptions may limit the effectiveness of the approach in certain scenarios, requiring careful experimentation and tuning.
    * **Computational cost:** It is unclear what the computational cost of using causal attention over standard attention mechanisms entails.

* **Potential Impact:**  If LEDIT proves to be robust across diverse datasets and scales, it could have a notable impact on the field of generative modeling, enabling more efficient and practical generation of high-resolution images. Further exploration and adoption by the research community are needed to fully realize its potential.
* **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and the concepts are clearly explained. However, a deeper investigation into the limitations of the theoretical derivation would further improve clarity.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**  The paper presents a genuinely interesting idea with solid empirical support. While the method offers a valuable pragmatic improvement, the improvement is relatively incremental over the current methods for length extrapolation. Further exploration and comparison of the computational cost of using causal attention should be made, and the approach should be tested on a larger range of datasets.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Large Language Models for Zero-shot Inference of Causal Structures in Biology
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04347v1)
- **Authors**: Izzy Newsham, Luka Kovačević, Richard Moulange, Nan Rosemary Ke, Sach Mukherjee
- **Abstract**: Genes, proteins and other biological entities influence one another via causal molecular networks. Causal relationships in such networks are mediated by complex and diverse mechanisms, through latent variables, and are often specific to cellular context. It remains challenging to characterise such networks in practice. Here, we present a novel framework to evaluate large language models (LLMs) for zero-shot inference of causal relationships in biology. In particular, we systematically evaluate causal claims obtained from an LLM using real-world interventional data. This is done over one hundred variables and thousands of causal hypotheses. Furthermore, we consider several prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, including large, and potentially conflicting, collections of scientific articles. Our results show that with tailored augmentation and prompting, even relatively small LLMs can capture meaningful aspects of causal structure in biological systems. This supports the notion that LLMs could act as orchestration tools in biological discovery, by helping to distil current knowledge in ways amenable to downstream analysis. Our approach to assessing LLMs with respect to experimental data is relevant for a broad range of problems at the intersection of causal learning, LLMs and scientific discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of large language models (LLMs) for zero-shot inference of causal relationships in biology, specifically gene regulatory networks. It benchmarks LLMs (using Gemma2-9B-it) on their ability to identify directed gene-gene causal relationships based on Perturb-seq data, comparing LLM predictions to a ground truth constructed from interventional experiments and traditional knowledge-driven approaches like the STRING database. The study evaluates various prompting and retrieval-augmentation strategies, including conditioning on biological context (cancer type, measurement modality, gene function) and incorporating literature extracts. The results suggest that even relatively small LLMs can capture meaningful aspects of causal structure with tailored prompting and augmentation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a timely and important question: can LLMs accelerate biological discovery by inferring causal relationships from literature and integrating with experimental data? Evaluating zero-shot causal inference in the context of gene regulation is a relatively unexplored area.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper employs a well-defined methodology for creating a causal ground truth from interventional data. It systematically explores different prompting strategies and compares LLM performance to a baseline method (STRING). This enables a controlled assessment of the LLM's capabilities.
*   **Relevance:** The research is highly relevant to the current trend of leveraging LLMs in scientific discovery. The focus on causal inference is particularly important, as it addresses a fundamental aspect of understanding biological systems.
*   **Contextual Focus:** The exploration of various contextual prompting strategies (cancer type, gene function, experimental details) is a valuable contribution. It highlights the importance of providing the LLM with appropriate biological and experimental context for effective inference.
*   **Detailed Experimentation:** The paper provides a detailed explanation of the LLM prompting techniques, allowing other researchers to follow or reproduce the study.
*   **Integration with Existing Resources:** Demonstrating the use of existing resources such as pubTator and STRING, the authors emphasize their findings in conjunction with well established tools.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited LLM Size:** The study is limited to a relatively small LLM (Gemma2-9B-it) due to computational constraints. It is unclear whether larger, more powerful LLMs would yield significantly better results. The authors acknowledge this but its impact cannot be understated.
*   **Zero-shot Setting:** While the zero-shot setting is valuable for assessing the inherent capabilities of LLMs, it may not reflect their full potential. Fine-tuning the LLM on biological data or incorporating reinforcement learning techniques could potentially improve performance.
*   **Ground Truth Limitations:** The ground truth is derived from Perturb-seq data, which has its own limitations (e.g., potential for off-target effects, context-specificity). The accuracy of the ground truth could impact the evaluation of the LLM.
*   **Modest Performance:** The AUROC score of 0.625, although better than random chance, suggests that the LLM's performance is still relatively modest. This raises questions about the practical utility of LLMs for zero-shot causal inference in biology.
*   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a specific type of causal relationship (gene-gene interactions) and a specific biological context (cancer cell lines). It is unclear whether the results generalize to other types of relationships or other biological systems.
*   **Lack of Clear Explanations:** While the paper presents the data in a concise and readily understandable manner, it is sometimes difficult to interpret precisely why changes in performance occur with specific additions to the prompts, or removal thereof.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in its early contribution to assessing the role of LLMs in causal structure learning for biology. It provides a valuable framework for evaluating LLM performance against experimental data and highlights the importance of contextual prompting. However, the modest performance and limitations in LLM size and scope suggest that further research is needed to realize the full potential of LLMs for biological discovery. The paper provides a framework for assessing the role of LLMs in this space. It is likely future LLMs can achieve significantly improved performance in such tasks.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel methodology and addresses a relevant problem. However, the modest performance of the LLM and the limitations in scope and ground truth quality prevent a higher score. The study serves as a valuable starting point for further research, but its immediate impact on biological discovery is likely to be limited. The methodological rigor and clear articulation of results are significant positives.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Layer-Specific Scaling of Positional Encodings for Superior Long-Context Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04355v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenghua Wang, Yiran Ding, Changze Lv, Zhibo Xu, Tianlong Li, Tianyuan Shi, Xiaoqing Zheng, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have achieved significant progress in handling long-context inputs, they still suffer from the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem, where crucial information in the middle of the context is often underrepresented or lost. Our extensive experiments reveal that this issue may arise from the rapid long-term decay in Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE). To address this problem, we propose a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method that assigns distinct scaling factors to each layer, slowing down the decay rate caused by RoPE to make the model pay more attention to the middle context. A specially designed genetic algorithm is employed to efficiently select the optimal scaling factors for each layer by incorporating Bezier curves to reduce the search space. Through comprehensive experimentation, we demonstrate that our method significantly alleviates the ``lost-in-the-middle'' problem. Our approach results in an average accuracy improvement of up to 20% on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset. Furthermore, we show that layer-specific interpolation, as opposed to uniform interpolation across all layers, enhances the model's extrapolation capabilities when combined with PI and Dynamic-NTK positional encoding schemes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the "lost-in-the-middle" problem in Large Language Models (LLMs) when processing long-context inputs. The authors propose a layer-specific positional encoding scaling method, adjusting the Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) decay rate in different layers to improve attention to the middle context. They use a genetic algorithm, constrained by Bézier curves, to efficiently find optimal scaling factors for each layer.  Their approach demonstrates improved accuracy on the Key-Value Retrieval dataset and MDQA dataset, and enhanced extrapolation capabilities when combined with Position Interpolation (PI) and Dynamic-NTK schemes. The paper also explores the underlying causes of the "lost-in-the-middle" issue, attributing it to both causal attention mechanisms and the long-term decay of RoPE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of layer-specific scaling of positional encodings is a moderate advance.  While previous work has considered scaling, this paper's contribution lies in the layer-specific optimization, which is justified by their observation that different layers exhibit distinct attention characteristics. The combination with Bézier curves and a genetic algorithm to optimize the scaling factors presents a practical, though not fundamentally novel, optimization technique. The exploration of the interplay between scaling and extrapolation is a valuable addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles an important, practical problem in LLM research – improving performance on long-context inputs. The experimental results, particularly the significant improvement on Key-Value Retrieval (+20%), are compelling. Showing improved extrapolation capabilities is also valuable. The method's claimed efficiency (not requiring per-head sensitivity calculations during inference) is a significant benefit compared to some prior work. However, the limitation to 7B models is a drawback as scaling behavior may vary with different model sizes. The paper's analysis of why layer-specific scaling helps, linking it to attention patterns and filtering roles of different layers, is also a good attempt to provide insight. However, the study does not explore the potential connections of this layer-specific scaling to the layer-specific specializations that are thought to occur in Transformers.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed experiments demonstrating performance improvements.
    *   Efficient optimization method for finding scaling factors.
    *   Insightful analysis of attention patterns and layer roles.
    *   Demonstration of enhanced extrapolation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited model size (7B).  The results might not generalize to larger models.
    *   The genetic algorithm, while practical, is not fundamentally a novel optimization technique.
    *   Some aspects of the analysis are somewhat speculative.
    *   Lack of thorough hyperparameter settings for the genetic algorithm (as per author's own limitations).
    *   The novelty of Bézier curves is more on the practical optimization side.
    *   The zero-shot results in ZeroScroll is fairly small (in terms of improvements), and so might not be fully reliable.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper offers a practical and relatively efficient way to improve the performance of LLMs on long-context tasks.  It could influence future research by encouraging more exploration of layer-specific techniques for optimizing LLM behavior and exploring connections between different layer scalings and the attention patterns within LLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, specifically in the practical realm of long-context LLM optimization.  It's not a paradigm shift, but a well-engineered solution to a known problem. The layer-specific aspect is the key differentiator, and it's supported by reasonable, though not entirely conclusive, analyses. The experimental gains are meaningful. However, the limitations regarding model size, the incremental nature of the optimization method (genetic algorithm and Bézier curves), and the somewhat small improvement on the zero-shot results on ZeroScroll are significant enough to preclude a higher score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04369v1)
- **Authors**: Yafu Li, Ronghao Zhang, Zhilin Wang, Huajian Zhang, Leyang Cui, Yongjing Yin, Tong Xiao, Yue Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in machine translation, demonstrating impressive performance across diverse languages. However, translationese, characterized by overly literal and unnatural translations, remains a persistent challenge in LLM-based translation systems. Despite their pre-training on vast corpora of natural utterances, LLMs exhibit translationese errors and generate unexpected unnatural translations, stemming from biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In this work, we systematically evaluate the prevalence of translationese in LLM-generated translations and investigate its roots during supervised training. We introduce methods to mitigate these biases, including polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that these approaches significantly reduce translationese while improving translation naturalness, validated by human evaluations and automatic metrics. Our findings highlight the need for training-aware adjustments to optimize LLM translation outputs, paving the way for more fluent and target-language-consistent translations. We release the data and code at https://github.com/yafuly/LLM_Translationese.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Lost in Literalism: How Supervised Training Shapes Translationese in LLMs" investigates the phenomenon of translationese in large language model (LLM)-based machine translation systems. Translationese refers to unnatural translations characterized by overly literal rendering of source text, even when LLMs are pre-trained on vast corpora of natural language. The authors systematically evaluate translationese in LLM outputs, finding its prevalence and tracing its roots to biases introduced during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). They propose mitigation strategies, including polishing golden references and filtering unnatural training instances. Empirical evaluations demonstrate that these approaches reduce translationese and improve translation naturalness, as validated by human evaluations and automatic metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Problem Focus:** The paper addresses a critical but often overlooked aspect of LLM-based translation: translation style and naturalness.  While adequacy is often emphasized, naturalness is equally important for creating fluent and user-friendly translations.
    *   **Systematic Investigation:** The study provides a systematic and quantitative analysis of translationese in LLMs, using both human evaluation and automatic metrics. The methodology involving expert annotators to identify translationese spans adds rigor.
    *   **Root Cause Analysis:** Identifying SFT as a source of translationese bias is a valuable contribution. It shifts the focus from solely pre-training data to considering the impact of the training paradigm.
    *   **Effective Mitigation Strategies:**  The proposed mitigation strategies, particularly polishing golden references, demonstrate practical ways to improve LLM translation quality. The improvements are validated by both human and automatic evaluations.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of data and code enhances the reproducibility and accessibility of the research.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Language Pairs:** The core evaluations are focused on English-Chinese and German-English, potentially limiting the generalizability to other language pairs, especially low-resource languages or languages with vastly different syntactic structures.
    *   **Reliance on Specific LLMs:** The study primarily utilizes specific LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen-2.5-7B, and GPT models).  While these are representative, exploring a wider range of models, particularly those designed with different architectures or training objectives, would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Subjectivity of Human Evaluation:** While expert annotators are used, the identification of translationese can still be somewhat subjective. The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the criteria used for annotation and measures taken to ensure inter-annotator reliability beyond reporting aggregate agreement scores. The lack of example annotations in the main paper is an oversight.
    *   **Over-emphasis on Faithfulness?** The finding that "translation" prompts inherently promote more literal translations, with less emphasis on generating natural, expressive output, is interesting. However, the emphasis on *faithfulness* may not be the *cause* of translationese, but instead a product of *specific* design decisions and/or datasets used during SFT.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *The paper is the first systematic study addressing translationese in LLMs.* Although prior works have looked at translationese in NMT and LLMs, this work uniquely identifies and addresses its causes in the context of LLM fine-tuning. It tackles the problem with concrete solutions that are experimentally validated.
    *The paper demonstrates the role of SFT in skewing the generative potential of otherwise expressive language models* towards a literal, less natural translationese.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting the problem of translationese in LLM-based translation, tracing its roots to SFT, and proposing practical mitigation strategies. The methodology is sound, and the results are supported by both human and automatic evaluations. The analysis and insights are valuable for the machine translation community. The primary weakness is the limited language scope and reliance on a few specific LLMs. Therefore, a score of **8** is assigned.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### TRACT: Regression-Aware Fine-tuning Meets Chain-of-Thought Reasoning for LLM-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04381v1)
- **Authors**: Cheng-Han Chiang, Hung-yi Lee, Michal Lukasik
- **Abstract**: The LLM-as-a-judge paradigm uses large language models (LLMs) for automated text evaluation, where a numerical assessment is assigned by an LLM to the input text following scoring rubrics. Existing methods for LLM-as-a-judge use cross-entropy (CE) loss for fine-tuning, which neglects the numeric nature of score prediction. Recent work addresses numerical prediction limitations of LLM fine-tuning through regression-aware fine-tuning, which, however, does not consider chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning for score prediction. In this paper, we introduce TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a method combining CoT reasoning with regression-aware training. TRACT consists of two stages: first, seed LLM is fine-tuned to generate CoTs, which serve as supervision for the second stage fine-tuning. The training objective of TRACT combines the CE loss for learning the CoT reasoning capabilities, and the regression-aware loss for the score prediction. Experiments across four LLM-as-a-judge datasets and two LLMs show that TRACT significantly outperforms existing methods. Extensive ablation studies validate the importance of each component in TRACT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRACT (Two-stage Regression-Aware fine-tuning with CoT), a novel method for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) in the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm. LLM-as-a-judge uses LLMs to automatically evaluate text, assigning scores based on rubrics. TRACT combines Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning with regression-aware training to improve the accuracy of numerical score prediction.  TRACT consists of two stages: first, a seed LLM is fine-tuned to generate CoTs, which then serve as supervision for the second fine-tuning stage. The training objective combines cross-entropy (CE) loss (for CoT reasoning) and a regression-aware loss (for score prediction). Experiments on four LLM-as-a-judge datasets and using two LLMs demonstrate that TRACT outperforms existing methods. The paper includes ablation studies that validate the importance of each component of TRACT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a technically sound and practical improvement to the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm. While combining CoT with regression is not entirely new, the two-stage approach to align the CoT distribution with the model and specifically addressing the mismatch between training (annotation CoTs) and inference (self-generated CoTs) shows thoughtful engineering. The two-stage fine-tuning approach is a notable contribution. The analysis showing CE alone with self-generated CoTs can be harmful highlights a subtlety often overlooked.

*   **Significance:**  The LLM-as-a-judge paradigm has become increasingly important for evaluating and improving LLMs. By enhancing the accuracy and reliability of LLMs in this role, TRACT makes a significant contribution. The consistent improvements across multiple datasets and models strengthens the paper's claims. The demonstration that TRACT can outperform even models trained on *more* data (Prometheus-2-7B which incorporates pairwise ranking data) under limited inference-time compute is particularly valuable, indicating real-world applicability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper provides extensive experimental results across four diverse datasets and two different LLMs, providing convincing evidence for the effectiveness of TRACT.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are crucial for understanding the contribution of each component of TRACT, particularly highlighting the importance of the self-generated CoTs and the CoT-RAFT objective.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details of TRACT, including the algorithm and training procedure. The figures are helpful for visualizing the method. The detailed hyperparameter settings are commendable for reproducibility.
    *   **Addressing a Practical Problem:**  The paper addresses a practical limitation of using LLMs for numerical score prediction and provides a solution that is both effective and relatively easy to implement.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on GPT-4:** The method still relies on GPT-4 (or a comparable model) for generating the initial CoT annotations. While the second stage uses self-generated CoTs, the performance depends on the quality of the initial annotations. The experiment demonstrating TRACT benefits from this first step relies on GPT-4 still limits its practical application.
    *   **Incremental Improvement?** While TRACT outperforms the baselines, the improvement over RAFT *alone* shows the biggest gains. The overall improvement while significant in some cases may be viewed as incremental, especially considering the added complexity.
    *   **Limited exploration of architectures/models:** Although two powerful language models were used to perform the experiments in this paper, additional experimentation with other open-source language models would give additional strength to the results outlined in this paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** TRACT has the potential to influence future research in the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm by providing a more effective method for fine-tuning LLMs for numerical score prediction. The findings regarding the importance of aligning CoT distributions could also be valuable for other applications of CoT reasoning.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper combines a regression loss with the COT methodology in a novel way which has resulted in improved results in language models. While the methodology has shown that it can have a noticeable improvement, the improvement may be limited by the complexity of the system, and may not be applicable or scalable due to its computational requirements. The code being available publicly is also helpful for others in the field.

**Score: 7.5**

*Rationale:* TRACT makes a solid contribution to the LLM-as-a-judge paradigm with a well-designed and experimentally validated method.  The insights regarding CoT distributions and the ablation studies add to the value. It falls short of being truly transformative due to the reliance on a powerful LLM for initial annotations and due to incremental improvements over previous methodologies. Additionally, the computational resources required is significant, especially given the two-stage methodology.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Shaping Shared Languages: Human and Large Language Models' Inductive Biases in Emergent Communication
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04395v1)
- **Authors**: Tom Kouwenhoven, Max Peeperkorn, Roy de Kleijn, Tessa Verhoef
- **Abstract**: Languages are shaped by the inductive biases of their users. Using a classical referential game, we investigate how artificial languages evolve when optimised for inductive biases in humans and large language models (LLMs) via Human-Human, LLM-LLM and Human-LLM experiments. We show that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge that enable reliable communication in all conditions, even when humans and LLMs collaborate. Comparisons between conditions reveal that languages optimised for LLMs subtly differ from those optimised for humans. Interestingly, interactions between humans and LLMs alleviate these differences and result in vocabularies which are more human-like than LLM-like. These findings advance our understanding of how inductive biases in LLMs play a role in the dynamic nature of human language and contribute to maintaining alignment in human and machine communication. In particular, our work underscores the need to think of new methods that include human interaction in the training processes of LLMs, and shows that using communicative success as a reward signal can be a fruitful, novel direction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how languages adapt to the inductive biases of their users, specifically humans and Large Language Models (LLMs). It employs a classical referential game and compares language evolution in Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM interaction scenarios. The study reveals that referentially grounded vocabularies emerge in all conditions, even when humans and LLMs collaborate. It further demonstrates that languages optimized for LLMs differ subtly from those optimized for humans, but Human-LLM interactions tend to create vocabularies that are more human-like than LLM-like. The authors argue that these findings underscore the importance of incorporating human interaction in the training processes of LLMs and suggest that using communicative success as a reward signal can be a valuable direction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the direct comparison of emergent languages in Human-Human, LLM-LLM, and Human-LLM collaborative settings within the same experimental framework. While prior work has explored language evolution with humans and LLMs separately, the cross-species interaction and the analysis of its impact on language structure and inductive biases are a significant contribution. It builds directly on previous work (Kouwenhoven et al., 2025) by extending the framework to include human participants.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for maintaining alignment and mutual understanding between humans and machines, particularly in the context of AI systems being increasingly integrated into daily life. The discovery that Human-LLM interactions can lead to more human-like languages suggests a potential pathway for developing AI systems that are better aligned with human cognitive preferences and communication styles. The paper strengthens the argument that focusing solely on AI-generated training data may lead to model collapse and the loss of human-understandable language structures, highlighting the value of human interaction in shaping AI language models.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined experimental setup:** The use of a classic referential game provides a controlled environment for studying language evolution.
    *   **Comparative analysis:** The direct comparison of different interaction conditions (Human-Human, LLM-LLM, Human-LLM) allows for a clear assessment of the influence of inductive biases.
    *   **Comprehensive metrics:** The use of a range of metrics, including PercCom, TopSim, and Ngram diversity, provides a detailed characterization of the emergent languages.
    *   **Clear and concise writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplicity of the Language Game:** The artificial language used in the experiment is deliberately simple. It is valid approach to understanding basic principles but might not fully translate to the complexities of natural language.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses solely on a referential game and does not consider other aspects of language use, such as argumentation, negotiation, or storytelling.
    *   **Generalizability to Different LLMs:** The paper focuses primarily on a single LLM (Llama 3 70B). The results might not generalize to other LLMs with different architectures or training data. While simulations are mentioned for other models in footnote, the analyses are not detailed.
    *   **LLMs as a Black Box:** While inductive biases are discussed, the inner workings of the LLM as they shape the languages remain somewhat of a black box, despite attempts to characterise languages using metrics.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of future AI systems by highlighting the importance of human interaction in language learning. The finding that communicative success can be used as a reward signal may inspire new training methods for LLMs. It also provides a strong rationale for incorporating insights from language evolution research into AI development.

**Overall:**

This is a well-executed and significant contribution to the field. The clear experimental design, comparative analysis, and insightful findings provide valuable insights into the interplay between human and machine inductive biases in language evolution. While some limitations exist in terms of the simplicity of the language game and the scope of the study, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The research moves beyond simply documenting LLM behavior towards exploring the dynamics of human-AI interaction and its impact on language. The results have direct relevance to developing better aligned and more human-understandable AI systems.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TableLoRA: Low-rank Adaptation on Table Structure Understanding for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04396v1)
- **Authors**: Xinyi He, Yihao Liu, Mengyu Zhou, Yeye He, Haoyu Dong, Shi Han, Zejian Yuan, Dongmei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Tabular data are crucial in many fields and their understanding by large language models (LLMs) under high parameter efficiency paradigm is important. However, directly applying parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques to tabular tasks presents significant challenges, particularly in terms of better table serialization and the representation of two-dimensional structured information within a one-dimensional sequence. To address this, we propose TableLoRA, a module designed to improve LLMs' understanding of table structure during PEFT. It incorporates special tokens for serializing tables with special token encoder and uses 2D LoRA to encode low-rank information on cell positions. Experiments on four tabular-related datasets demonstrate that TableLoRA consistently outperforms vanilla LoRA and surpasses various table encoding methods tested in control experiments. These findings reveal that TableLoRA, as a table-specific LoRA, enhances the ability of LLMs to process tabular data effectively, especially in low-parameter settings, demonstrating its potential as a robust solution for handling table-related tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "TableLoRA: Low-rank Adaptation on Table Structure Understanding for Large Language Models" introduces TableLoRA, a specialized module designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to understand and process tabular data during parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT).  TableLoRA addresses two key challenges: better table serialization using special tokens and representing two-dimensional structured information in a one-dimensional sequence. It incorporates a special token encoder for serializing tables and a 2D LoRA to encode low-rank information about cell positions (row and column indices).  The authors conduct experiments on four table-related datasets, demonstrating that TableLoRA outperforms vanilla LoRA and surpasses other table encoding methods. They present ablation studies and analyses to understand the efficacy of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of a table-specific LoRA module is somewhat novel.  While prior work explores table understanding with LLMs and PEFT techniques, TableLoRA's design to incorporate special tokens and 2D LoRA specifically for structural information is a differentiator. However, the individual components (LoRA, special tokens, positional embeddings) are not novel in themselves; the novelty lies in the specific combination and application to table understanding within the PEFT framework.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem: improving LLMs' performance on tabular data in resource-constrained settings. Tables are prevalent, and the ability to efficiently process them with LLMs is valuable. TableLoRA's demonstrated improvements over vanilla LoRA on multiple datasets suggest its potential as a practical solution. The ablation studies contribute to understanding the relative importance of the special token encoder and the 2D LoRA components.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and the experimental setup.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, including comparisons with baselines, ablation studies, and analyses of different table complexities.
    *   The paper identifies and addresses specific challenges related to table understanding with LLMs in PEFT.
    *   The authors provide insights into how TableLoRA enhances row-column correspondence and hierarchical header understanding.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking.  It builds upon existing techniques.
    *   While the paper mentions computational resource savings, the specific energy consumption implications of TableLoRA compared to full fine-tuning or other methods could be explored in more detail.
    *   The experiments are limited to a specific set of datasets and models. Testing on a broader range of models, especially larger and more powerful ones, would strengthen the findings.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations in handling queries involving arithmetic operations, suggesting a need for further research in that area.
    *  The control experiment in 4.3.1 is lacking, because "add in attention mask" and "Add in positional embedding" are both tested solely (i.e. row/column indices embeddings are only added to word embeddings or the attention masks, without the 2D LoRA as shown in Figure 2). This makes it more difficult to show that 2D LoRA actually "works".

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in table understanding with LLMs, especially in PEFT settings. It could serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated table-specific adaptation techniques. The insights into the importance of structural information and the effectiveness of special tokens and 2D positional encoding could guide future research.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and weighing the incremental novelty against the practical significance of the problem addressed and the empirical results presented, I assign a score of 6.5.  While the paper offers a worthwhile contribution to the field, it leverages existing techniques in a relatively straightforward manner. The experimental results are encouraging, but further validation on a wider range of models and datasets is needed. The limitations in handling arithmetic queries also suggest avenues for future research.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Speculative MoE: Communication Efficient Parallel MoE Inference with Speculative Token and Expert Pre-scheduling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04398v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Li, Pengfei Zheng, Shuang Chen, Zewei Xu, Yunfei Du, Zhengang Wang
- **Abstract**: MoE (Mixture of Experts) prevails as a neural architecture that can scale modern transformer-based LLMs (Large Language Models) to unprecedented scales. Nevertheless, large MoEs' great demands of computing power, memory capacity and memory bandwidth make scalable serving a fundamental challenge and efficient parallel inference has become a requisite to attain adequate throughput under latency constraints. DeepSpeed-MoE, one state-of-the-art MoE inference framework, adopts a 3D-parallel paradigm including EP (Expert Parallelism), TP (Tensor Parallel) and DP (Data Parallelism). However, our analysis shows DeepSpeed-MoE's inference efficiency is largely bottlenecked by EP, which is implemented with costly all-to-all collectives to route token activation. Our work aims to boost DeepSpeed-MoE by strategically reducing EP's communication overhead with a technique named Speculative MoE. Speculative MoE has two speculative parallelization schemes, speculative token shuffling and speculative expert grouping, which predict outstanding tokens' expert routing paths and pre-schedule tokens and experts across devices to losslessly trim EP's communication volume. Besides DeepSpeed-MoE, we also build Speculative MoE into a prevailing MoE inference engine SGLang. Experiments show Speculative MoE can significantly boost state-of-the-art MoE inference frameworks on fast homogeneous and slow heterogeneous interconnects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Speculative MoE" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Speculative MoE (s-MoE), a technique aimed at improving the efficiency of Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) inference.  s-MoE addresses the communication bottleneck inherent in Expert Parallelism (EP) by proactively predicting expert routing paths for tokens. This is achieved through two main mechanisms: speculative token shuffling (s-TS) and speculative expert grouping (s-EG).  s-TS pre-shuffles tokens to devices where their predicted experts reside, while s-EG pre-clusters experts likely to be activated by related tokens. The approach is integrated into both DeepSpeed-MoE and SGLang frameworks, demonstrating performance improvements across various MoE models, datasets, and hardware configurations (homogeneous and heterogeneous interconnects). The paper also includes several engineering optimizations such as fused kernels and deduplication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of *speculatively* reducing communication in MoE inference is incremental. While previous works have explored expert prefetching/offloading and some forms of expert placement (e.g., ExFlow), the combination of s-TS and s-EG, along with their specific implementations within popular frameworks, and the use of a balanced co-clustering technique constitutes a significant engineering advance.  The use of probabilistic models for token-expert route prediction, and modeling both intra-layer and inter-layer affinity is valuable, even if it is based on a tabularized conditional probability approach. The concept of online speculative token shuffling and offline expert pre-grouping is a sound engineering decision.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates practical improvements in MoE inference throughput under different latency constraints (TTFT, TPOT, p90-TBT).  The results showing gains on both fast homogeneous (NVLink) and slow heterogeneous (PCIe) interconnects are especially important as it makes the technique relevant to a broad range of deployment scenarios. The integration and benchmarking in both DeepSpeed-MoE and SGLang underscores the technique's generalizability. The analysis on the effectiveness of s-TS vs. s-EG helps in understanding the impact of different components and suggests potential for further optimization.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across different models, datasets, and hardware.
    *   Integration and validation in multiple popular frameworks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies and analysis of contributing factors.
    *   Practical system optimizations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The probabilistic models for predicting token-expert routes, although effective, are relatively simple.  More sophisticated prediction methods could potentially yield further gains.
    *   The description of some implementation details could be more thorough (e.g., the specifics of the co-clustering algorithm in Appendix B).
    *   While the paper claims to reduce EP communication volume losslessly, the accuracy of token-expert route prediction is less than perfect, implying potential for performance degradation in some extreme cases. This aspect could be further investigated.

*   **Impact:** The paper has a high potential to influence the field of MoE inference, especially as MoE models become more prevalent. The techniques presented are practical and readily deployable in existing frameworks.  It provides a valuable contribution towards democratizing LLM inference, particularly for setups with resource constraints.

**Justification for Score:**

The score reflects that while s-MoE is not a revolutionary breakthrough in MoE theory, it is a significant *practical* advancement. It takes existing principles and concepts (expert prefetching, token re-ordering) and combines them with careful engineering and system-level optimizations to achieve tangible performance benefits. The paper's strengths lie in its thorough evaluation, practical focus, and integration into established frameworks, making it immediately useful to researchers and practitioners working on MoE deployment. The weaknesses are mostly related to the relative simplicity of prediction models and less-than-perfect route predictions that need more detailed analysis. It builds nicely on ExFlow by incorporating inter-layer affinity and adding support for intra-layer affinity, leading to better pre-scheduling. Overall, the novelty, significance, and influence of this work point toward a strong contribution to the community.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Wider or Deeper? Scaling LLM Inference-Time Compute with Adaptive Branching Tree Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04412v1)
- **Authors**: Kou Misaki, Yuichi Inoue, Yuki Imajuku, So Kuroki, Taishi Nakamura, Takuya Akiba
- **Abstract**: Recent advances demonstrate that increasing inference-time computation can significantly boost the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Although repeated sampling (i.e., generating multiple candidate outputs) is a highly effective strategy, it does not leverage external feedback signals for refinement, which are often available in tasks like coding. In this work, we propose $\textit{Adaptive Branching Monte Carlo Tree Search (AB-MCTS)}$, a novel inference-time framework that generalizes repeated sampling with principled multi-turn exploration and exploitation. At each node in the search tree, AB-MCTS dynamically decides whether to "go wider" by expanding new candidate responses or "go deeper" by revisiting existing ones based on external feedback signals. We evaluate our method on complex coding and engineering tasks using frontier models. Empirical results show that AB-MCTS consistently outperforms both repeated sampling and standard MCTS, underscoring the importance of combining the response diversity of LLMs with multi-turn solution refinement for effective inference-time scaling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Adaptive Branching Monte Carlo Tree Search (AB-MCTS), a new inference-time framework for large language models (LLMs). AB-MCTS aims to improve reasoning capabilities by combining repeated sampling with multi-turn exploration and exploitation. Unlike standard MCTS or beam search with fixed branching factors, AB-MCTS dynamically decides at each node in the search tree whether to "go wider" (generate new candidate responses) or "go deeper" (refine existing responses) based on external feedback signals. The decision process is formalized using Bayesian posterior updates. The authors evaluate AB-MCTS on complex coding and machine learning engineering tasks using state-of-the-art LLMs and demonstrate that it outperforms repeated sampling and standard MCTS.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core idea of adaptively balancing exploration and exploitation within an MCTS framework for LLM inference is somewhat novel.  While MCTS has been used with LLMs before, the dynamic adjustment of branching factor based on feedback signals distinguishes this work.  The use of Bayesian updates to formalize this decision-making process adds a layer of sophistication. However, adaptive branching exists within the general MCTS literature; therefore the novelty comes from the application to LLMs rather than invention of the entire algorithm.

* **Significance:**  Inference-time scaling is a crucial area of research, as it provides a path to improve LLM performance without retraining. The paper addresses a key limitation of repeated sampling, which is its lack of explicit exploitation. By incorporating external feedback, AB-MCTS potentially opens the door to more effective problem-solving in scenarios where such feedback is available (e.g., coding, debugging).  The empirical results, showing consistent outperformance of established baselines, reinforces the significance of this approach.  The paper also identifies a weakness of existing LLM inference approaches: that a fixed branching factor in tree search neglects LLM's vast output space. By proposing to solve this issue, it identifies the importance of LLM output space when improving inference time.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of balancing exploration and exploitation in LLM inference.
    * **Well-Defined Algorithm:** AB-MCTS is presented in a well-structured and understandable manner.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experiments are conducted on complex tasks and demonstrate consistent performance gains over strong baselines (repeated sampling, standard MCTS). The evaluation uses leading-edge models (GPT-4o, DeepSeek-V3).
    * **Two Variants:** Providing two AB-MCTS variant widens the scope of the research.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Task Domain:** The evaluation focuses primarily on coding and MLE tasks. It would be helpful to see how AB-MCTS performs in other domains where external feedback is readily available (e.g., mathematical reasoning).
    * **Reliance on Feedback Quality:**  The performance of AB-MCTS is inherently tied to the quality and reliability of the external feedback signals. The paper could benefit from a discussion of how AB-MCTS behaves when feedback is noisy or incomplete. A sensitivity analysis could further strengthen the claims.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The choice of priors for the Bayesian updates in AB-MCTS likely impacts its performance. While the authors describe their choices, a more thorough exploration of hyperparameter sensitivity would be beneficial.
    * **Runtime Complexity:** MCTS methods in general are computationally expensive. More details are needed about the runtime complexity relative to repeated sampling and standard MCTS to understand scalability.

* **Potential Impact:** AB-MCTS could influence future research on inference-time scaling for LLMs, particularly in tasks where external feedback is available. It provides a framework for combining the benefits of exploration (response diversity) and exploitation (solution refinement). The results suggest that adaptive branching can lead to more efficient use of computational resources.

**Overall Assessment:**

This is a solid paper that introduces a novel and well-motivated approach to inference-time scaling for LLMs. The empirical results are compelling, and the algorithm is clearly presented. The weaknesses outlined above, such as the limited task domain and hyperparameter sensitivity, are areas for future research but do not detract significantly from the paper's overall contribution.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** While MCTS is not new, the *adaptive* branching strategy informed by external feedback, combined with a Bayesian formulation, offers a clear advance over existing LLM inference methods, warranting the score to be on the higher side. The core algorithm has novelty, is evaluated well empirically with state-of-the-art models, and could have a high impact on the field. The aforementioned weaknesses, limit a higher rating, as they could be addressed with more research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can Large Language Models Predict Antimicrobial Resistance Gene?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04413v1)
- **Authors**: Hyunwoo Yoo
- **Abstract**: This study demonstrates that generative large language models can be utilized in a more flexible manner for DNA sequence analysis and classification tasks compared to traditional transformer encoder-based models. While recent encoder-based models such as DNABERT and Nucleotide Transformer have shown significant performance in DNA sequence classification, transformer decoder-based generative models have not yet been extensively explored in this field. This study evaluates how effectively generative Large Language Models handle DNA sequences with various labels and analyzes performance changes when additional textual information is provided. Experiments were conducted on antimicrobial resistance genes, and the results show that generative Large Language Models can offer comparable or potentially better predictions, demonstrating flexibility and accuracy when incorporating both sequence and textual information. The code and data used in this work are available at the following GitHub repository: https://github.com/biocomgit/llm4dna.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of generative Large Language Models (LLMs) for DNA sequence analysis, specifically focusing on predicting antimicrobial resistance genes. It compares the performance of generative models (LLaMA 3.1, Claude 3.5, ChatGPT 4o-mini) to traditional encoder-based models used in bioinformatics, such as DNABERT, in terms of classification accuracy. The study explores how the performance of LLMs changes when provided with additional textual information extracted from BLASTn searches and when using fine-tuning techniques (LoRA). The results suggest that generative LLMs can achieve comparable or potentially better performance than traditional methods, especially when incorporating supplementary textual data. The study also demonstrates the ability of LLMs to handle multiple labels and adapt to different labeling environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The application of generative LLMs to DNA sequence analysis, specifically for predicting antimicrobial resistance genes, represents a moderate level of novelty. While encoder-based models like DNABERT are well-established in the field, the exploration of LLMs *specifically* for this type of task is less common, though not entirely absent (as cited by the authors with BioGPT). The core novelty lies in the investigation of how LLMs can leverage additional textual information from tools like BLASTn alongside sequence data, a potentially advantageous capability compared to models that primarily rely on sequence embeddings. However, the novelty is somewhat diminished by the existence of prior work exploring LLMs for bioinformatics tasks.

* **Significance:** The significance of the work is moderate. The paper provides empirical evidence that LLMs can perform well in this domain, particularly when incorporating supplementary information. This suggests a promising avenue for future research and potential applications in clinical settings for rapid identification of resistance genes. The ability of LLMs to handle multiple labels also addresses a challenge in genomic analysis where a single sequence may have various classifications. However, the study's impact is somewhat limited by the relatively small scale of the dataset and the lack of a direct comparison to state-of-the-art encoder models specifically designed for DNA sequence classification. Also, the improvement is observed by using information from the BLAST database. So, LLMs would be simply automating and improving the use of already existing databases.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Demonstrates the feasibility of using generative LLMs for a practical bioinformatics task.
    *   Highlights the advantage of LLMs in incorporating additional textual information.
    *   Explores the ability of LLMs to handle multi-label classification.
    *   Provides a comparison of different LLMs (LLaMA 3.1, Claude 3.5, ChatGPT 4o-mini).

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset size is relatively small, limiting the generalizability of the findings.
    *   Lacks a robust comparison with optimized, state-of-the-art encoder-based models like DNABERT2 fine-tuned for the specific AMR classification task. The comparison to DNABERT would give a direct baseline for the field.
    *   The "unclassified rate" metric is somewhat unconventional and less informative than standard precision/recall/F1 scores across *all* classes.
    *   The method used for extracting labels from LLM outputs isn't clearly described.
    *   The paper is relatively exploratory and lacks a strong theoretical framework explaining *why* LLMs are better able to incorporate the BLASTn information.
    *   The benefit could be simply leveraging the Blast database not the LLMs, because these LLMs have a higher level of performance only by using the data of Blastn search results, making the LLMs simply more efficient.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could encourage further research into the application of LLMs in genomics and bioinformatics. It may also inspire the development of new methods for integrating textual and sequence data for improved genomic analysis.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents an interesting application of generative LLMs in a bioinformatics context. However, it has several limitations in terms of dataset size, comparison to baselines, and depth of analysis. The novelty is moderate, and the significance is somewhat limited by these factors. The results provide a starting point for further research, but the study itself doesn't represent a major breakthrough. The reliance on external databases reduces the impact of the LLMs on their own.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### Learning Transformer-based World Models with Contrastive Predictive Coding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04416v1)
- **Authors**: Maxime Burchi, Radu Timofte
- **Abstract**: The DreamerV3 algorithm recently obtained remarkable performance across diverse environment domains by learning an accurate world model based on Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs). Following the success of model-based reinforcement learning algorithms and the rapid adoption of the Transformer architecture for its superior training efficiency and favorable scaling properties, recent works such as STORM have proposed replacing RNN-based world models with Transformer-based world models using masked self-attention. However, despite the improved training efficiency of these methods, their impact on performance remains limited compared to the Dreamer algorithm, struggling to learn competitive Transformer-based world models. In this work, we show that the next state prediction objective adopted in previous approaches is insufficient to fully exploit the representation capabilities of Transformers. We propose to extend world model predictions to longer time horizons by introducing TWISTER (Transformer-based World model wIth contraSTivE Representations), a world model using action-conditioned Contrastive Predictive Coding to learn high-level temporal feature representations and improve the agent performance. TWISTER achieves a human-normalized mean score of 162% on the Atari 100k benchmark, setting a new record among state-of-the-art methods that do not employ look-ahead search.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a rigorous critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces TWISTER, a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm that learns a transformer-based world model augmented with action-conditioned Contrastive Predictive Coding (AC-CPC).  TWISTER aims to improve the agent's ability to learn high-level temporal feature representations, leading to enhanced performance in challenging RL environments. The core idea is to leverage AC-CPC to extend world model predictions to longer time horizons. This allows the agent to better understand the consequences of its actions and learn more effective policies. The authors demonstrate that TWISTER achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Atari 100k benchmark compared to other model-based methods that don't employ look-ahead search. Ablation studies explore the impact of AC-CPC components and design choices. Furthermore, the algorithm is also applied to a standard continuous control benchmark and showed better performance in complex control tasks.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the *specific* combination of Transformer-based world models with AC-CPC in the reinforcement learning context. While Transformers and CPC have been explored individually in RL, this particular integration and its action-conditioned variant is a key contribution. Previous transformer-based world models primarily focused on next-state prediction, which, as the authors argue, is insufficient to capture long-term dependencies. AC-CPC provides a mechanism for learning representations that are sensitive to future actions and states. While the paper builds upon existing work like STORM, it addresses a critical limitation by improving feature representation learning with the use of AC-CPC and improving performance in games with small moving objects. The proposed approach also shows significant improvement in standard continues control tasks.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its improved performance on the Atari 100k benchmark and its potential to push the boundaries of sample-efficient RL. The results indicate that AC-CPC is an effective method for learning high-quality representations in world models. The improvement over methods like STORM, which also use Transformer-based world models, highlights the value of AC-CPC. Sample efficiency is a major bottleneck in RL, and advances in this area are highly valuable. The demonstrated success in Atari, a standard but challenging benchmark, adds credibility to the approach. Also, the fact the performance is improved over DreamerV3 on several challenging continuous control benchmark is promising. The method could influence future research in model-based RL, particularly in the design of world models and feature learning objectives.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   Strong empirical results on a standard benchmark.
    *   Thorough ablation studies that provide insights into the importance of different components.
    *   Well-written and easy-to-understand paper.
    *   Code and the model are released.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper focuses on a specific set of environments (Atari and DeepMind Control Suite). While these are standard benchmarks, it is important to consider the limitations of these environments and the generalizability of the findings to other domains.
    *   Although the method addresses the issue of small objects, the improvement relies on the appropriate setting of hyperparameters related to the number of contrastive steps.
    *   Although the work outperforms other non-look-ahead world models, it is still not competitive with more advanced methods using search (e.g., MuZero).
    *   Limited discussion on computational overhead compared to previous approaches. Although Transformers are known for their training efficiency, the introduction of AC-CPC may add additional computational costs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

    The paper demonstrates a clear improvement over existing methods on the Atari 100k benchmark and exhibits sound empirical methodology with detailed ablation studies.  The use of AC-CPC in this particular way is novel, and the results suggest a promising direction for future research. However, the weaknesses related to generalizability, comparison to planning-based methods, and the limited discussion on computational overhead prevent a higher score.
