- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Compositional Translation: A Novel LLM-based Approach for Low-resource Machine Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04554v1)
- **Authors**: Armel Zebaze, Benoît Sagot, Rachel Bawden
- **Abstract**: The ability of generative large language models (LLMs) to perform in-context learning has given rise to a large body of research into how best to prompt models for various natural language processing tasks. Machine Translation (MT) has been shown to benefit from in-context examples, in particular when they are semantically similar to the sentence to translate. In this paper, we propose a new LLM-based translation paradigm, compositional translation, to replace naive few-shot MT with similarity-based demonstrations. An LLM is used to decompose a sentence into simpler phrases, and then to translate each phrase with the help of retrieved demonstrations. Finally, the LLM is prompted to translate the initial sentence with the help of the self-generated phrase-translation pairs. Our intuition is that this approach should improve translation because these shorter phrases should be intrinsically easier to translate and easier to match with relevant examples. This is especially beneficial in low-resource scenarios, and more generally whenever the selection pool is small or out of domain. We show that compositional translation boosts LLM translation performance on a wide range of popular MT benchmarks, including FLORES 200, NTREX 128 and TICO-19. Code and outputs are available at https://github.com/ArmelRandy/compositional-translation
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Compositional Translation: A Novel LLM-based Approach for Low-resource Machine Translation" introduces a new translation paradigm called "compositional translation" (CompTra). This method aims to improve machine translation performance, particularly in low-resource scenarios, by decomposing sentences into simpler phrases, translating these phrases using retrieved in-context examples, and then recombining the translated phrases to generate the final translation.  The core idea is that LLMs are more effective at translating shorter, well-formed phrases and that it's easier to find relevant in-context examples for such segments.  Experiments conducted on English-to-X (low resource) MT show that CompTra consistently outperforms few-shot translation with similarity-based example selection (BM25, SONAR) across various language directions, LMs (LLAMA, GEMMA, Command).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of exploiting compositionality in machine translation isn't entirely new. The paper acknowledges existing decomposition-based approaches. However, the specific instantiation of CompTra, its reliance on LLMs for both decomposition and translation, and the integration of similarity-based retrieval for phrase translation demonstrate novelty. The self-generation aspect also offers distinct approach when compared to approaches that relies on external resources such as dictionaries. The use of LLMs to identify the decomposition as well makes the appraoch hyperparameter free.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance lies in addressing a persistent challenge in machine translation: low-resource scenarios. While large pre-trained models have significantly improved MT, their performance often degrades when translating into languages with limited data. CompTra offers a way to leverage LLM capabilities more effectively in such situations, and the evaluation results show improvements over existing strategies. The ablations provide further important details about why CompTra is able to achieve improved performances.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents extensive experimental results across multiple datasets (FLORES 200, NTREX 128, TICO-19) and low-resource languages, demonstrating the effectiveness of CompTra.
    *   **Clear and Well-Defined Methodology:** The steps involved in CompTra are clearly outlined, making it easy to understand and potentially replicate.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The paper includes ablation studies to examine the impact of different components of CompTra, providing insights into the importance of phrase quality, the retriever, and the decomposition strategy.
    *  **Discussion and analysis**: Comprehensive analysis of results is presented including the discussion of weak components, hyperparameter choice and potential weaknesses.
    *   **Out-of-domain robustness:** Results showcase that CompTra is still effective even when out-of-domain.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The approach heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. While the paper demonstrates CompTra's effectiveness with specific LLMs, the performance might vary depending on the choice of LLM and its inherent biases. More analysis on the limitations given the size of the model is required.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper shows faster results given no sequential nature, it is not clear how much computational cost is required given the multiple steps of decomposition, translation, and recomposition, compared to standard MT approaches.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Decomposition Strategies:** Although the ablation studies explore different strategies, there might be even more effective decomposition approaches that were not considered.
    *   **Lack of High-Resource Languages Analysis**: While there exist some analysis on higher resource languages, there is no thorough analysis to indicate how CompTra would perform on high resource MT directions and scenarios.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis is Limited**: Only few examples are presented to highlight the benefits of the method to the reader.

*   **Potential Influence:** CompTra has the potential to influence future research in low-resource MT by providing a viable alternative to traditional approaches. It also encourages further exploration of compositionality and reasoning-based approaches for MT with LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

CompTra is a novel and well-executed approach to improve MT performance in low-resource scenarios. The paper presents compelling experimental evidence to support the effectiveness of the method and provides valuable insights into its underlying mechanisms through ablation studies. Its integration of LLMs for both decomposition and translation, and use of self-generation adds a significant level of novelty. However, the reliance on LLM quality, some limitations in analysis and the limited exploration of other decomposition strategies limit its overall score. But the paper paves the way for a compositional approach towards MT and provides useful information that could support the field.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HybridNorm: Towards Stable and Efficient Transformer Training via Hybrid Normalization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04598v1)
- **Authors**: Zhijian Zhuo, Yutao Zeng, Ya Wang, Sijun Zhang, Jian Yang, Xiaoqing Li, Xun Zhou, Jinwen Ma
- **Abstract**: Transformers have become the de facto architecture for a wide range of machine learning tasks, particularly in large language models (LLMs). Despite their remarkable performance, challenges remain in training deep transformer networks, especially regarding the location of layer normalization. While Pre-Norm structures facilitate easier training due to their more prominent identity path, they often yield suboptimal performance compared to Post-Norm. In this paper, we propose $\textbf{HybridNorm}$, a straightforward yet effective hybrid normalization strategy that integrates the advantages of both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm approaches. Specifically, HybridNorm employs QKV normalization within the attention mechanism and Post-Norm in the feed-forward network (FFN) of each transformer block. This design not only stabilizes training but also enhances performance, particularly in the context of LLMs. Comprehensive experiments in both dense and sparse architectures show that HybridNorm consistently outperforms both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm approaches, achieving state-of-the-art results across various benchmarks. These findings highlight the potential of HybridNorm as a more stable and effective technique for improving the training and performance of deep transformer models. %Code will be made publicly available. Code is available at https://github.com/BryceZhuo/HybridNorm.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "HybridNorm," a novel normalization strategy for training transformers. HybridNorm combines the benefits of both Pre-Norm and Post-Norm architectures. It employs QKV normalization within the attention mechanism and Post-Norm in the feed-forward network (FFN) of each transformer block. The authors claim this hybrid approach leads to more stable training and improved performance, particularly in large language models (LLMs).  They present empirical results on dense and sparse architectures showing HybridNorm outperforms Pre-Norm and Post-Norm across various benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The *idea* of combining different normalization techniques isn't entirely new. Prior work has explored adaptive normalization or mixing Pre-Norm and Post-Norm at different layers (e.g., Mix-LN mentioned in the paper). The specific *combination* of QKV normalization in attention and Post-Norm in FFN is, however, a distinct contribution.  The paper lacks a clear and thorough discussion of the existing landscape and how HybridNorm carves out a truly unique space, making the novelty appear incremental rather than groundbreaking. The related work section could be strengthened by better contextualizing HybridNorm with respect to similar normalization strategies and architectural modifications.

*   **Significance:** Improving the training stability and performance of transformers, especially for LLMs, is a significant problem. If HybridNorm delivers on its promise, it could lead to more efficient training runs, better models, and reduced computational costs. The empirical results are promising. The performance gains across various benchmarks, particularly in LLM-relevant tasks (BasicArithmetic, HellaSwag, ARC-Easy, ARC-Challenge and OpenbookQA), suggest practical value. However, the paper lacks a strong theoretical underpinning. A more rigorous analysis of *why* this specific combination works, perhaps connecting to gradient flow or regularization effects, would significantly enhance the paper's impact. The theoretical gradient analysis included in the Appendix could be expanded and better integrated to support their claims.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity:** HybridNorm is relatively straightforward to implement, making it accessible to researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides solid empirical evidence supporting its claims, with experiments on both dense and sparse architectures, and across various benchmarks.
    *   **Focus on LLMs:** The paper's specific focus on LLMs makes the results relevant to a rapidly evolving and high-impact area.
    *   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of individual components (e.g., different initialization strategies).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** The paper lacks a deep theoretical explanation of why the specific HybridNorm combination of QKV Normalization in MHA and Post-Norm in FFN works well, beyond the intuitive idea of combining benefits. This limits the generalizability and potential for future research.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the specific combination is new, the high-level idea of hybrid normalization is less novel.
    *   **Presentation:** The writing can be somewhat dense, and the organization could be improved to better highlight the key contributions and differentiate HybridNorm from existing techniques.
    *   **Comparison with Mix-LN:** While acknowledged in remark 3.1, it could benefit from a more in-depth comparison with Mix-LN and a discussion of how it improves over it and what its advantages are
*   **Long-Term Impact:**  If HybridNorm's benefits hold up in more extensive testing, it could become a standard building block for transformer architectures. The simplicity of the approach is a positive factor for adoption. However, the lack of a strong theoretical foundation and the incremental nature of the novelty compared to existing solutions might limit its long-term influence. It is possible that this is a well-engineered practical improvement that will get adopted and yield real benefits.

**Justification of Score:**

While the empirical results are encouraging and the focus on LLMs is relevant, the paper's novelty is somewhat incremental, and its theoretical foundation is weak. There are prior works that have also used normalization in different layers of the transformer network. To justify a higher score, the paper would need a more in-depth theoretical analysis or a clear demonstration of significant advantages over existing hybrid normalization techniques. It is a decent engineering contribution and that will probably have some practical use.

### The Best of Both Worlds: Integrating Language Models and Diffusion Models for Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04606v1)
- **Authors**: Aoxiong Yin, Kai Shen, Yichong Leng, Xu Tan, Xinyu Zhou, Juncheng Li, Siliang Tang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in text-to-video (T2V) generation have been driven by two competing paradigms: autoregressive language models and diffusion models. However, each paradigm has intrinsic limitations: language models struggle with visual quality and error accumulation, while diffusion models lack semantic understanding and causal modeling. In this work, we propose LanDiff, a hybrid framework that synergizes the strengths of both paradigms through coarse-to-fine generation. Our architecture introduces three key innovations: (1) a semantic tokenizer that compresses 3D visual features into compact 1D discrete representations through efficient semantic compression, achieving a $\sim$14,000$\times$ compression ratio; (2) a language model that generates semantic tokens with high-level semantic relationships; (3) a streaming diffusion model that refines coarse semantics into high-fidelity videos. Experiments show that LanDiff, a 5B model, achieves a score of 85.43 on the VBench T2V benchmark, surpassing the state-of-the-art open-source models Hunyuan Video (13B) and other commercial models such as Sora, Keling, and Hailuo. Furthermore, our model also achieves state-of-the-art performance in long video generation, surpassing other open-source models in this field. Our demo can be viewed at https://landiff.github.io/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LanDiff, a novel text-to-video (T2V) generation framework designed to combine the strengths of both autoregressive language models (LLMs) and diffusion models. It addresses the limitations of each paradigm by using an LLM for high-level semantic understanding and causal modeling and then a diffusion model for refining the visual details. Key innovations include: 1) a semantic tokenizer that compresses 3D visual features into compact 1D discrete representations, achieving a high compression ratio; 2) a language model generating semantic tokens with high-level relationships; and 3) a streaming diffusion model refining coarse semantics into high-fidelity videos. Experiments on the VBench T2V benchmark demonstrate that LanDiff achieves state-of-the-art performance compared to open-source models, including long-video generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The central idea of combining LLMs and diffusion models for T2V isn't entirely novel, as other hybrid approaches exist. However, LanDiff's novelty lies in its specific architecture and the design choices made for each component. The semantic tokenizer, with its high compression rate and emphasis on semantic information, is a significant contribution. The streaming diffusion model tailored for long-video generation is also a valuable addition.
* **Significance:** The paper demonstrates impressive results, surpassing existing open-source models on established benchmarks. This suggests a real advance in T2V generation. The high compression ratio of the tokenizer could have implications beyond T2V, potentially benefiting other video processing tasks. The modular design allows for potential improvements to individual components (LLM, diffusion model, tokenizer) without drastically changing the overall architecture. The demonstration of state-of-the-art performance in *long* video generation is a key strength.
* **Strengths:**
    *   The hybrid architecture addresses the key limitations of both LLM-based and diffusion-based T2V models.
    *   The semantic tokenizer achieves a remarkably high compression ratio while preserving essential semantic information.
    *   The streaming diffusion model is well-suited for long-video generation, mitigating computational costs.
    *   Extensive experimental results demonstrate superior performance on VBench and VBench-Long, surpassing many open and closed sourced models.
    *   The detailed explanation of implementation choices aids in reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper claims superiority to closed-source models like Sora, Keling, and Hailuo, the evaluation is based on publicly reported numbers, which may not be entirely comparable due to differences in training data or evaluation methodologies. A direct, side-by-side comparison on a held-out dataset would strengthen the claim.
    *   Although the paper mentions the control conditions (frames, motion score), more details on their impact on the generation process and their limitations would enhance understanding.
    *  The tokenizer appears to be heavily dependent on Theia which makes reproducibility more challenging as this model is not readily available.
    * The qualitative results are good but do suffer from some temporal inconsistency even though this is an advantage of using LLMs in generating video.

* **Potential Impact:** The work has the potential to influence future research in T2V generation. The high compression ratio of the semantic tokenizer, the hybrid architecture, and the streaming diffusion model offer valuable insights for researchers exploring more efficient and semantically rich video generation techniques. If the code and model are released, it would significantly accelerate progress in the field.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a genuinely interesting and effective method for T2V generation, addressing inherent limitations of existing paradigms. The core strength is the architectural design that thoughtfully combines LLMs and diffusion models and how it leads to demonstrable improvements in quality and long-video generation. The performance leap is compelling, especially given the relatively modest model size (5B).

However, there are limitations. The reliance on potentially not fully accessible and reproducible data for closed source models makes it difficult to assess the true extent of its superiority. The lack of a thorough ablation study exploring all potential control parameter settings also holds the paper back. While there is ablation with and without the tokenizer and classifier free guidance, there are other control parameters discussed. A more comprehensive analysis is needed to justify a very high score. Finally, even though LLMs should help with temporal consistency, there are qualitative issues that raise some concerns.

Despite these weaknesses, the significant improvements on existing benchmarks, the novel architecture, and the thorough analysis justify a relatively high score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Data-Efficient Language Models: A Child-Inspired Approach to Language Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04611v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammad Amin Ghanizadeh, Mohammad Javad Dousti
- **Abstract**: In this work, we explain our approach employed in the BabyLM Challenge, which uses various methods of training language models (LMs) with significantly less data compared to traditional large language models (LLMs) and are inspired by how human children learn. While a human child is exposed to far less linguistic input than an LLM, they still achieve remarkable language understanding and generation abilities. To this end, we develop a model trained on a curated dataset consisting of 10 million words, primarily sourced from child-directed transcripts. The 2024 BabyLM Challenge initial dataset of 10M words is filtered to 8.5M. Next, it is supplemented with a randomly selected subset of TVR dataset consisting of 1.5M words of television dialogues. The latter dataset ensures that similar to children, the model is also exposed to language through media. Furthermore, we reduce the vocabulary size to 32,000 tokens, aligning it with the limited vocabulary of children in the early stages of language acquisition. We use curriculum learning and is able to match the baseline on certain benchmarks while surpassing the baseline on others. Additionally, incorporating common LLM training datasets, such as MADLAD-400, degrades performance. These findings underscore the importance of dataset selection, vocabulary scaling, and curriculum learning in creating more data-efficient language models that better mimic human learning processes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes an approach to training language models (LMs) with significantly less data, inspired by how children learn language.  The authors focus on three key strategies: (1) Curating a dataset of 10 million words primarily from child-directed transcripts, supplemented with television dialogue; (2) Scaling the vocabulary size down to 32,000 tokens; and (3) Using curriculum learning to present progressively more complex linguistic inputs.  The experiments explore the impact of these strategies, including comparisons with and without television data, the effect of different vocabulary sizes, and the benefits of curriculum learning. The paper finds that a curated dataset with television dialogue, a vocabulary size of 32,000 tokens, and curriculum learning improves performance.  Incorporating a larger, more general dataset like MADLAD-400, however, degrades performance. The authors posit that data valuation and attribution methods could be useful for future data selection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of data-efficient language models inspired by child language acquisition isn't entirely new, and the BabyLM Challenge itself centers on this concept. However, the specific combination of strategies (dataset curation focusing on child-directed speech and media, vocabulary scaling *to a specific token count, and curriculum learning implemented with their described complexity scoring*)  and the detailed exploration of each component contribute some novelty. The negative result with the MADLAD-400 dataset is a particularly interesting finding.  The curriculum learning approach, while building on existing work, features specific scoring functions, contributing a minor but noticeable innovative element.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its empirical investigation into what constitutes effective data for small language models. The result that a large, general dataset *degrades* performance highlights that quantity isn't always better than quality, and *especially not better than relevance*. The specific findings regarding vocabulary size and curriculum learning offer practical insights for researchers working on data-efficient LMs.  The idea of using data valuation and attribution methods is a promising avenue for future research.  The work helps to refine our understanding of what aspects of the data and training process are most important for achieving good performance with limited resources.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined research question:** The paper clearly states its goals and approach.
    * **Systematic experimentation:**  The experiments are designed to isolate the impact of different factors.
    * **Interesting findings:** The results, particularly the negative impact of MADLAD-400, are insightful.
    * **Clear writing:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on the BLIMP and GLUE benchmarks. More extensive evaluation on diverse downstream tasks would strengthen the claims.
    * **Hyperparameter sensitivity:** The results might be sensitive to the specific hyperparameters used.  A more thorough hyperparameter search could improve performance and robustness. While the paper mentions the weights used in curriculum learning, it acknowledges that finding near-optimal values can be computationally expensive, presenting a limitation to their findings.
    * **Incremental contribution:** While the findings are interesting, they are incremental and not revolutionary.  The paper primarily provides empirical evidence rather than a fundamentally new theoretical framework.
    * **Limited analysis of error cases:** A more detailed analysis of the specific errors made by the different models would provide deeper insights into the effectiveness of the different strategies.

* **Potential influence:**  The paper could influence researchers working on data-efficient LMs, particularly those interested in mimicking human language acquisition.  The insights regarding data curation, vocabulary size, and curriculum learning can inform the design of future experiments.  The negative result with MADLAD-400 could lead researchers to be more cautious about blindly using large datasets. However, the overall impact may be limited due to the incremental nature of the contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid piece of empirical work that contributes to our understanding of data-efficient language models. However, its novelty is limited, and the overall impact is likely to be incremental. While the negative result with MADLAD-400 is valuable, and the exploration of various strategies provides useful insights, the paper doesn't offer a groundbreaking new framework or theory. A more rigorous exploration of downstream tasks, hyperparameter sensitivity, and error analysis would have significantly strengthened the paper. Therefore, while a worthwhile contribution, a higher score is not warranted.
